) and Proposition
3.8
, we know that for any
s<t

\int\int\int_{s}^{t}|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{d}^{p}(r)drd\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}_{\Lambda}(\lambda)=\int\int_{s}^{t}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\mu}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(r)drd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\int_{s}^{t}|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(r)dr.

Using Lebesgue theorem, we easily obtain that for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
it holds

\int\int|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{d}^{p}(t)d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}_{\Lambda}(\lambda)=\int|\dot{\boldsymbol{\mu}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(t)d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})=|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)dt.

Moreover, from Proposition
3.6
, for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
it holds

|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)dt\leq\int\int|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{d}^{p}(t)d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda).

∎

3.6.
Geodesics of random measures

In this subsection, we want to give a characterization for the
geodesics
in the space of random measures
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
. Assume that
(X,d)
is a complete, separable and geodesic metric space. It is well known that, under these assumptions on
X
, the space
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X),W_{p})
is geodesic as well, for
p>1
(see e.g.
[
ABS24
, Theorem 10.6]
). Reiterating it, we already know that
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
is geodesic.

With the notation introduced in §
2.1.3
, thanks to Corollary
A.9
, we define

(3.21)

\displaystyle\hfill\operatorname{GEO}:\mathcal{P}(

\displaystyle X\times X)\to\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X)),\quad\operatorname{GEO}(\pi):=\operatorname{geo}_{\sharp}\pi,\hfill

\displaystyle\hfill\operatorname{GEO}_{t}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\to\mathcal{P}(X),\quad\operatorname{GEO}_{t}(\pi):=(\operatorname{geo}_{t})_{\sharp}\pi.\hfill

Notice that, since
\operatorname{geo}
is not Borel measurable in general, we cannot use Proposition
D.8
to conclude that
\operatorname{GEO}
(and
\operatorname{GEO}_{t}
) is measurable. One way to deal with this would be to obtain
\operatorname{GEO}
again using a measurable selection argument. For the sake of the presentation, we do not enter into details and we stick with the previous definitions, that work well whenever the geodesics are uniquely determined so that
\operatorname{geo}
and
\operatorname{GEO}
are Borel measurable.

On the other hand, we denote by
\operatorname{geo}_{\mathcal{P}_{p}}:\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)\times\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)\to\operatorname{Geo}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
the map defined in (
2.6
) in the geodesic space
\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)
. Similarly,
\operatorname{geo}_{t,\mathcal{P}_{p}}
is its evaluation at time
t
, for all
t\in[0,1]
.

First, we show how a geodesic connecting two given random measures
M_{0},M_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
can be obtained either from
\Pi\in\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
or
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
(see §
3.1
).

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
M_{0},M_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
. If
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
, then
M_{t}^{\mathfrak{P}}:=(\operatorname{GEO}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}
is a geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
connecting
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
.

If
\Pi\in\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
, then
M_{t}^{\Pi}:=(\operatorname{geo}_{t,\mathcal{P}_{p}})_{\sharp}\Pi
is a geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
connecting
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
.

Proof.

Regarding the first claim, for all
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle(M_{s}^{\mathfrak{P}},M_{t}^{\mathfrak{P}})\leq\int\int d^{p}(\operatorname{geo}_{s}(x,y),\operatorname{geo}_{t}(x,y))d\pi(x,y)d\mathfrak{P}(\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-s)^{p}\int\int d^{p}(x,y)d\pi(x,y)d\mathfrak{P}(\pi)=(t-s)^{p}\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1}).

Similarly for the second claim:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle(M_{s}^{\Pi},M_{t}^{\Pi})\leq\int W_{p}^{p}(\operatorname{geo}_{s,\mathcal{P}}(\mu,\nu),\operatorname{geo}_{t,\mathcal{P}}(\mu,\nu))d\Pi(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-s)^{p}\int W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)d\Pi(\mu,\nu)=(t-s)^{p}\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1}).

∎

The next proposition completely characterizes geodesics in terms of their liftings
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X)))
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],\mathcal{P}(X)))
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in C([0,1],\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)))
. The following are equivalent

(1)

\boldsymbol{M}
is a geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
;

(2)

there exists
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X)))
lifting of
\boldsymbol{M}
, concentrated over
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X))
that are, in turn, supported over
\operatorname{Geo}(X)
, and such that
(E_{0,1})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
, where

(3.22)

E_{0,1}:\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X))\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X),\quad E_{0,1}(\lambda):=(e_{0},e_{1})_{\sharp}\lambda;

(3)

there exists
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],\mathcal{P}(X)))
lifting of
\boldsymbol{M}
that is supported on
\operatorname{Geo}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
and such that
(\mathfrak{e}_{0},\mathfrak{e}_{1})_{\sharp}\Lambda\in\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
.

Proof.

(1)
\implies
(2):
\boldsymbol{M}
being a geodesic implies that
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC_{1}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)))
. Then, consider any
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X)))
built as in Proposition
3.9
, and we show it shares the wanted properties. By construction, it is a lifting of
\boldsymbol{M}
. Regarding the geodesic property, it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1})\leq

\displaystyle\int\int d^{p}(x_{0},x_{1})d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\leq\int\int\left(\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|(r)dr\right)^{p}d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\int\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{p}(r)drd\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(r)dr=\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1}),

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{M}
is a geodesic and the second last one from Proposition
3.9
. The previous computation forces all inequalities to be equalities. In particular, the first implies that
(E_{0,1})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
, while the second and the third one imply, respectively, that for
\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda
and for
\lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{x}
, it holds

d(x_{0},x_{1})=\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|(r)dr\quad\text{ and }\quad|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|\text{ is constant},

that together imply that
\boldsymbol{x}\in\operatorname{Geo}(X)
.

(2)
\implies
(3): define
\Lambda:=E_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}
. It is immediate to verify that, if
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,1],X))
is supported over
\operatorname{Geo}(X)
and
(e_{0},e_{1})_{\sharp}\lambda
is optimal, then
\boldsymbol{\mu}:=E(\lambda)\in\operatorname{Geo}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
. We conclude observing
(\mathfrak{e}_{0},\mathfrak{e}_{1})_{\sharp}\Lambda=P_{\sharp}\big((E_{0,1})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}\big)
, where
P
is defined as in (
3.6
).

(3)
\implies
(1): thanks to Proposition
3.8
and the assumptions, it holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1})\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|^{p}(r)dr\leq\int\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\mu}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(r)drd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1}).

Reasoning as above, it implies that
\boldsymbol{M}
is a constant speed geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)),\mathcal{W}_{p})
.
∎

The previous result can be improved when
X=\mathbb{R}^{d}
(for simplicity, but the same holds whenever
X
is a Polish space for which there is uniqueness of geodesics), showing that all the objects involved are unique whenever we restrict ourselves to either
[0,t]
or
[t,1]
, for
t\in(0,1)
, giving a non-branching property for geodesics of random measures.

First we need the operation of composition of random couplings, following
[
AGS08
, Lemma 5.3.2]
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
X
be a Polish space, and
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
. Let
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma(M_{1},M_{2})
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2,3}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma(M_{2},M_{3})
. Then, there exists
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X\times X\times X))
such that

(3.23)

P^{1,2}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}=\mathfrak{P}_{1,2},\quad P^{2,3}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}=\mathfrak{P}_{2,3},

where, for all
i,j=1,2,3

P^{i,j}:\mathcal{P}(X\times X\times X)\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X),\quad P^{i,j}(\theta)=p^{i,j}_{\sharp}\theta.

In particular,
P^{1,3}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma(M_{1},M_{3})
.

Proof.

The proof again uses a measurable selection argument. Let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\times\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
be the Borel subset defined as

\mathcal{A}=\left\{(\pi,\sigma)\in\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\times\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\,:\,p^{2}_{\sharp}\pi=p^{1}_{\sharp}\sigma\right\}.

Define the map

\mathrm{P}=(P^{1,2},P^{2,3}):\mathcal{P}(X\times X\times X)\to\mathcal{A},\quad\mathrm{P}(\theta):=(p^{1,2}_{\sharp}\theta,p^{2,3}_{\sharp}\theta).

It is Borel thanks to Proposition
D.8
. Moreover, thanks to
[
AGS08
, Lemma 5.3.2 & Remark 5.3.3]
, the map
\mathrm{P}
is surjective. Thus, we can apply Theorem
A.10
to obtain a Souslin-Borel measurable map
\mathrm{Q}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X\times X)
that is a right-inverse of
\mathrm{P}
.

Now, consider the disintegration of
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2,3}
, respectively, with respect to the maps
P^{1}
and
P^{2}
, to obtain that

\mathfrak{P}_{1,2}=\int_{\mathcal{P}}\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,\mu}dM_{t}(\mu),\quad\mathfrak{P}_{2,3}=\int_{\mathcal{P}}\mathfrak{P}_{2,3,\mu}dM_{t}(\mu),

where
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,\mu}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X\times X))
is concentrated over couplings
\pi
for which
p^{2}_{\sharp}\pi=\mu
, for
M_{t}
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, and similarly for
\mathfrak{P}_{2,3,\mu}
. Notice that, for
M_{t}
-a.e.
\mu
, the product measure
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,\mu}\otimes\mathfrak{P}_{2,3,\mu}
is concentrated over
\mathcal{A}
. Thus, we can define

(3.24)

\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}:=\mathrm{Q}_{\sharp}\left(\int_{\mathcal{P}}\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,\mu}\otimes\mathfrak{P}_{2,3,\mu}dM_{t}(\mu)\right)=\int_{\mathcal{P}}\mathrm{Q}_{\sharp}\left(\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,\mu}\otimes\mathfrak{P}_{2,3,\mu}\right)dM_{t}(\mu),

It is not hard to show that
\mathfrak{P}_{1,2,3}
shares the wanted properties.
∎

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in C([0,1],\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
be a geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\mathcal{W}_{p})
. Then, for every
t\in(0,1)
,
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{t})
(resp.
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{t},M_{1})
) contains a unique optimal random coupling
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}
(resp.
\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}
).

Moreover, there exists a unique
\mathfrak{L}_{0,t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{d})))
(resp.
\mathfrak{L}_{t,1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C([t,1],\mathbb{R}^{d})))
) lifting of
(M_{s})_{s\in[0,t]}
(resp.
(M_{s})_{s\in[t,1]}
) satisfying property (2) in Proposition
3.11
.

Similarly, there exists a unique
\Lambda_{0,t}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,t],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
(resp.
\Lambda_{t,1}\in\mathcal{P}(C([t,1],\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))))
) lifting of
(M_{s})_{s\in[0,t]}
(resp.
(M_{s})_{s\in[t,1]}
) satisfying property (3) in Proposition
3.11
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{t})
and
\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{t},M_{1})
be any optimal random coupling. Applying Proposition
3.12
, we obtain
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfying

(3.25)

P^{1,2}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}=\mathfrak{P}_{0,t},\quad P^{2,3}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}=\mathfrak{P}_{t,1},\quad\mathfrak{P}_{0,1}:=P^{1,3}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma(M_{0},M_{1}).

Exploiting the geodesic property, we can also show that
\mathfrak{P}_{0,1}\in\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{0}(M_{0},M_{1})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}

\displaystyle(M_{0},M_{1})\leq\left(\iint|x-y|^{p}d\pi(x,y)d\mathfrak{P}_{0,1}(\pi)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\left(\iint|x_{1}-x_{3}|^{p}d\theta(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})d\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}(\theta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\iint|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{p}d\theta(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})d\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}(\theta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\iint|x_{2}-x_{3}|^{p}d\theta(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})d\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}(\theta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}(M_{0},M_{t})+\mathcal{W}_{p}(M_{t},M_{1})=\mathcal{W}_{p}(M_{0},M_{1}).

An important property is also hidden in the previous computation: since the last inequality is actually an equality, we know that for
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}
-a.e.
\theta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for
\theta
-a.e.
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, the three points are aligned, in the sense that there exists
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
x_{2}-x_{1}=\alpha(x_{3}-x_{1})
. Moreover, using again the geodesic property, it is not hard to show that
\alpha=t
. In particular, for
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}
-a.e.
\theta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for
\theta
-a.e.
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
, it holds
x_{1}=\frac{x_{2}-tx_{3}}{1-t}
and
x_{3}=\frac{x_{2}-(1-t)x_{1}}{t}
. Thus, define the functions

\ell_{1}(x_{2},x_{3}):=\left(\frac{x_{2}-tx_{3}}{1-t},x_{2},x_{3}\right),\quad\mathrm{L}_{1}:=(\ell_{1})_{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\ell_{3}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\left(x_{1},x_{2},\frac{x_{2}-(1-t)x_{1}}{t}\right),\quad\mathrm{L}_{3}:=(\ell_{3})_{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Thanks to the previous observations, we can conclude that
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}=P^{1,2}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t,1}=P^{1,2}_{\sharp}\big((\mathrm{L}_{1})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}\big)
, that makes us conclude that
\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}
is unique, since it has been chosen independently of
\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}
. Similarly, the uniqueness for
\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}
is implied by
\mathfrak{P}_{t,1}=P^{2,3}_{\sharp}\big((\mathrm{L}_{3})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}\big)
.

The uniqueness of
\mathfrak{L}_{0,t}
follows from Proposition
3.11
and observing that the required properties force it to be equal to
P^{0,t}_{\sharp}\mathfrak{P}_{0,t}
, where

P^{0,t}:=p^{0,t}_{\sharp},\quad p^{0,t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to C([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ p^{0,t}(x_{1},x_{2})=[s\mapsto(1-s)x_{1}+sx_{2}].

The uniqueness of
\Lambda_{0,t}
now follows, and similarly, the same holds for
\mathfrak{L}_{t,1}
and
\Lambda_{t,1}
.
∎

3.7.
Barycenters

In this subsection, we apply our result to show that, if
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC^{p}_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X)))
, then the curves of its barycenters is in
AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}(X))
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X))
. The barycenter of
M
is the measure
\operatorname{bar}[M]\in\mathcal{P}(X)
defined such that it satisfies

(3.26)

\int_{X}f(x)d\big(\operatorname{bar}[M]\big)(x)=\int\int_{X}f(x)d\mu(x)dM(\mu)

for all Borel functions
f:X\to[0,+\infty]
.

Recalling the definition of
\widetilde{M}\in\mathcal{P}(X\times\mathcal{P}(X))
given in Remark
2.6
, we notice that the first marginal of
\widetilde{M}
is indeed
\operatorname{bar}[M]
. Now, we show a nice property of the barycenter with respect to the nested push-forward described in (
2.9
).

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
X,Y
be two Polish spaces,
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X))
and
f:X\to Y
a Borel map. Define the map
F:\mathcal{P}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
as the push-forward of
f
, i.e.
F=f_{\sharp}
. Then it holds

(3.27)

\operatorname{bar}[F_{\sharp}M]=f_{\sharp}(\operatorname{bar}[M]).

Proof.

Let
N=F_{\sharp}M
. For all
g:Y\to[0,+\infty]
Borel measurable, thanks to (
2.10
), it holds

\displaystyle\int_{Y}g(y)d\operatorname{bar}[N](y)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\int_{Y}g(y)d\nu(y)dN(\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\int_{X}g(f(x))d\mu(x)dM(\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}g(f(x))d\big(\operatorname{bar}[M]\big)(x)=\int_{Y}g(y)df_{\sharp}(\operatorname{bar}[N])(y).

∎

Given a curve
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X)))
, we indicate with
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{bar}[M]}=(\operatorname{bar}[M_{t}])_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(X))
the curve of the barycenters.

Proposition 3.16
.

If
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC^{p}_{T}\big(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))\big)
, then
\boldsymbol{m}:=\boldsymbol{\operatorname{bar}[M]}\in AC^{p}_{T}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
, and, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, it holds

(3.28)

|\dot{\boldsymbol{m}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(t)\leq\int\int|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{p}(t)d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t).

Proof.

For all
t\in[0,T]
,
\operatorname{bar}[M_{t}]\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(X)
, indeed

\int_{X}d^{p}(x,\bar{x})d\operatorname{bar}[M_{t}](x)=\int\int_{X}d^{p}(x,\bar{x})d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)<+\infty.

Consider
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{A}_{p}}\big(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(X))\big)
defined as
\mathfrak{L}:=(G_{\sharp})\circ\operatorname{Lift}(\boldsymbol{M})
. Define the map

\displaystyle(E_{t},E_{s}):\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(X))

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X)

\displaystyle\lambda\

\displaystyle\mapsto(e_{t},e_{s})_{\sharp}\lambda,

and
\mathfrak{P}_{t,s}:=(E_{t},E_{s})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\big)
. Notice that the barycenter of
\mathfrak{P}_{t,s}
, indicated as
\pi_{t,s}\in\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
, is a coupling between
\operatorname{bar}[M_{t}]
and
\operatorname{bar}[M_{s}]
: using Lemma
3.15
, indeed
\operatorname{bar}[(E_{t})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}]=(e_{t})_{\sharp}(\operatorname{bar}[\mathfrak{L}])
, and same for
s
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(\operatorname{bar}[M_{t}]

\displaystyle,\operatorname{bar}[M_{s}])\leq\int\int d^{p}(x,y)d\pi(x,y)d\mathfrak{P}_{t,s}(\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int d^{p}(\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s})d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\leq|t-s|^{p-1}\int_{s}^{t}\int\int|\dot{\gamma}|^{p}(r)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)dr,

which implies that
\boldsymbol{m}:=\boldsymbol{\operatorname{bar}[M]}\in AC_{T}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(X))
, and using Lebesgue theorem, it holds

(3.29)

|\dot{\boldsymbol{m}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(t)\leq\int\int|\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}|^{p}(t)d\lambda(\boldsymbol{x})d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)\quad\text{for a.e. $t\in[0,T]$.}

∎

4.
Continuity equation on random measures

4.1.
Derivations, continuity equation and a first superposition result

Derivations are the natural objects that can be used to define an abstract continuity equation over a metric space
X
, see
[
ST17
]
. Here, we adapt this definition in the case
X=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, endowed with the narrow topology.

Definition 4.1

(Cylinder functions and Wasserstein gradient)
.

A functional
F:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is called a
C^{1}_{c}
-cylinder function if there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Phi=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k})\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\Psi\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that

(4.1)

F(\mu)=\Psi\left(L_{\Phi}(\mu)\right),\quad L_{\Phi}(\mu)=\big(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\phi_{k}}(\mu)\big),\quad L_{\phi_{i}}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{i}(x)d\mu(x).

Its Wasserstein gradient is then defined as

(4.2)

\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{i}\Psi\left(L_{\Phi}(\mu)\right)\nabla\phi_{i}(x)\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The collection of all the cylinder functions is called
\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. If
F=\Psi\circ L_{\Phi}
with
\Phi\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\Psi\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, we say that
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}_{b}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Notice that, when
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we can consider the outer function
\Psi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Moreover, we have the inclusion
\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\subset\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}_{b}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Remark 4.2
.

Since
\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)=\nabla_{x}\left(\frac{d^{+}}{d\varepsilon}|_{\varepsilon=0}\ F((1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\delta_{x})\right)
, the Wasserstein gradient does not depend on the representation chosen for
F
.

Definition 4.3

(
L^{p}
derivations)
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
p\geq 1
. An
L^{p}(M)
-derivation is a linear operator
B:\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to L^{p}(M)
such that

(4.3)

B[FG]=GB[F]+FB[G]\quad M\text{-a.e.}

and there exists a non-negative function
c\in L^{p}(M)
such that

(4.4)

|B[F](\mu)|\leq c(\mu)\|\nabla_{W}F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu)}\quad\forall F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ \text{ for }M\text{-a.e. }\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
p^{\prime}
is the conjugate exponent of
p
.

We will extensively work with families of
L^{p}
-derivations: to be more specific, let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be a Borel family of random measures and
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
a family of
L^{p}(M_{t})
-derivations such that
(t,\mu)\mapsto B_{t}[F](\mu)
is Borel measurable for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and there exists a non-negative function
c\in L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
(recall the notation introduced in §
2.3.1
) such that

(4.5)

|B_{t}[F](\mu)|\leq c_{t}(\mu)\|\nabla_{W}F\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu)}\ M_{t}\otimes dt\text{-a.e.}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}c_{t}^{p}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty.

We will refer to such a kind of family of derivations as
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivations. Notice that, since all the measures involved are probability measures, an
L^{p}(M)
-derivation is also an
L^{q}(M)
-derivation for each
q\in[1,p]
.

Remark 4.4
.

Thanks to (
4.4
), given a representative of
c
, there exists
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
M(\mathcal{N})=0
and
|B[F](\mu)|\leq c(\mu)\|\nabla_{W}F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu)}<+\infty
for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\mu\in\mathcal{N}^{c}
.
Similarly for
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivations, exploiting (
4.5
), there exists
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times[0,T]
such that
M_{t}\otimes dt(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})=0
and
|B_{t}[F](\mu)|\leq c_{t}(\mu)\|\nabla_{W}F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu)}<+\infty
for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
(\mu,t)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{c}
.

Definition 4.5

(Continuity equation on random measures)
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a continuous curve of Borel probability measures over
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
. Let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a family of
L^{1}(M_{t})
-derivations, according to (
4.5
). We say that the continuity equation

(4.6)

\frac{d}{dt}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}\big(B_{t}M_{t}\big)=0

is satisfied if

(4.7)

\displaystyle\forall F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\quad\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{P}}F(\mu)\ dM_{t}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}}B_{t}[F](\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)

in the sense of distributions in
(0,T)
, where
\mathcal{P}
is short for
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 4.6
.

The assumption that the curve
t\mapsto M_{t}
is continuous, is not restrictive. Indeed, if such curve is just Borel measurable, thanks to Lemma
C.7
, we can always find a (unique) continuous representative of it.

Before proceeding, we state and prove two technical, but useful, lemmas.

Lemma 4.7

(Chain rule)
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
B
be an
L^{1}(M)
-derivation. Then, for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
of the form
F=\Psi(L_{\Phi}(\mu))
as in (
4.1
), for
M
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds

(4.8)

B[F](\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{i}\Psi(L_{\Phi}(\mu))B[L_{\phi_{i}}](\mu).

Proof.

Thanks to the Leibniz rule, by linearity and induction (
4.8
) holds when
\Psi
is a polynomial. When
\Psi
is not a polynomial, consider
(p_{n})
a sequence of polynomial approximating
\Psi
uniformly on compact sets, together with its first derivatives. By the boundedness of
\Phi
and its first derivatives, we conclude.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
such that
(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Let
p\geq 1
. Let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a family of
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivations and
c\in L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
as in (
4.5
).
Then the functions

(4.9)

(t,\boldsymbol{\mu})\mapsto B_{t}[F](e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu})),\quad(t,\boldsymbol{\mu})\mapsto c_{t}^{p}(e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))

are
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\Lambda
-measurable and well-defined. Moreover, for
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\Lambda
-a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{\mu})
and for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, it holds

(4.10)

|B_{t}[F](e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))|\leq c_{t}(e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))|\nabla_{W}F(\cdot,e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))}<+\infty,\quad\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}(e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))dt<+\infty.

Proof.

The functions in (
4.9
) are composition of measurable maps, so they are measurable. Now, consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
as in Remark
4.4
and define the function

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}:[0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

\displaystyle\to[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle(t,\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle\mapsto(t,\mu_{t}),

where we mean that
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, so that
\mu_{t}=e_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
.
Notice that
\widetilde{E}_{\sharp}(\mathcal{L}_{T}^{1}\otimes\Lambda)=dt\otimes M_{t}
, so
\widetilde{E}^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}})
is a negligible set w.r.t.
\mathcal{L}_{T}^{1}\otimes\Lambda
, which implies (
4.10
). To conclude, notice that

\int\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dtd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\int_{0}^{T}\int c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}c_{t}^{p}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty.

∎

Now, we prove a first superposition result in terms of derivations.
Our proof strongly relies on the techniques developed in
[
AT14
]
: indeed, we embed the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, where an infinite-dimensional version of Theorem
2.12
holds (see Appendix
B
).

We are going to use a similar notation as for the purely metric setting:

•

\boldsymbol{\gamma}:=(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

•

\lambda\in\mathcal{P}\big(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
;

•

\boldsymbol{\mu}:=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}\big(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
;

•

M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
;

•

\boldsymbol{M}:=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}\big(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\big)
;

•

\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}\big(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\big)
;

•

\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\big)
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a continuous curve of probability measures over
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a Borel family of derivations and
c\in L^{1}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
as in (
4.5
) with
p=1
.
Assume that
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(B_{t}M_{t})=0
in the sense of Definition
4.5
.
Then there exists a probability measure
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}\big(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, such that

•

(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
;

•

for
\Lambda
-a.e. curve
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,
B_{t}[F](\mu_{t})
and
c_{t}(\mu_{t})
are well defined and it holds

(4.11)

\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}(\mu_{t})dt<+\infty\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}\big(B_{t}\mu_{t}\big)=0\quad\text{for }\Lambda\text{-a.e. }(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}

in the sense of distributions in duality with
C_{c}^{1}
functions, i.e. for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)

(4.12)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi^{\prime}(t)\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt=-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)B_{t}[L_{\phi}](\mu_{t})dt.

Proof.

Step 1
: here we use the result presented in Appendix
B
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots\}\subset C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\iota
as in Appendix
B
, i.e.

•

\phi_{k}
is Lipschitz with respect to
|\cdot|\wedge 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
\|\nabla\phi_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
;

•

\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{A})
dense in
C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e. functions that, together with their first derivatives, converge to
0
at infinity;

•

\hat{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu):=W_{1,|\cdot|\wedge 1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{k}d(\mu-\nu)
;

and

(4.13)

\iota:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\quad\iota(\mu)=\big(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),L_{\phi_{2}}(\mu),\dots\big).

Step 2
: define
\mathtt{m}_{t}:=\iota_{\sharp}M_{t}
. We prove that
(\mathtt{m}_{t})\in AC_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}),W_{1,D_{\infty}})
(see (
B.1
)).

For any
s,t\in[0,T]
, consider
\Pi_{s,t}
an optimal transport plan between
M_{t}
and
M_{s}
realizing
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(M_{t},M_{s}):=W_{1,\hat{W}_{1}}(M_{t},M_{s})
. Then
(\iota,\iota)_{\sharp}\Pi_{t,s}
is a transport plan between
\mathtt{m}_{t}
and
\mathtt{m}_{s}
, so that

\displaystyle W_{1,D_{\infty}}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{m}_{t},\mathtt{m}_{s})\leq\int D_{\infty}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\ d(\iota,\iota)_{\sharp}\Pi_{t,s}(x,y)=\int\sup_{k}|L_{\phi_{k}}(\mu)-L_{\phi_{k}}(\nu)|\wedge 1\ d\Pi_{t,s}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\sup_{k}|L_{\phi_{k}}(\mu)-L_{\phi_{k}}(\nu)|d\Pi_{t,s}(\mu,\nu)=\int\hat{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)d\Pi_{t,s}(\mu,\nu)=\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(M_{t},M_{s}),

and we are done thanks to Lemma
C.7
.

Step 3
: define, component-wisely, the vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
as

(4.14)

v_{t}^{(k)}(\mathrm{x}):=\begin{cases}B_{t}[L_{\phi_{k}}](\iota^{-1}(\mathrm{x}))&\text{ if }\mathrm{x}\in\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\\
0&\text{ if }\mathrm{x}\notin\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).\end{cases}

Notice that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}|v_{t}^{(k)}(\mathrm{x})|d\mathtt{m}_{t}(\mathrm{x})dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}|B_{t}[L_{\phi_{k}}](\mu_{t})|dM_{t}(\mu)dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}c_{t}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty,

and for any cylinder function
F:\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e. such that there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which
F(\mathrm{x})=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, it holds (thanks to Lemma
4.8
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}F(\mathrm{x})d\mathtt{m}_{t}(\mathrm{x})=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{P}}F(\iota(\mu))dM_{t}(\mu)=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{P}}F(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu))dM_{t}(\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\partial_{i}F(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu))\ B_{t}[L_{\phi_{k}}](\mu_{t})dM_{t}(\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}\nabla F(\mathrm{x})\cdot\big(v_{t}^{(1)},\dots,v_{t}^{(n)}\big)d\mathtt{m}_{t}(\mathrm{x}).

Then, we can apply Theorem
B.5
, to obtain the existence of a measure
\mathtt{L}\in\mathcal{P}\big(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},d_{\infty})\big)
satisfying

(e_{t})_{\sharp}\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{m}_{t}

and
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}\in C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
is weakly absolutely continuous with

\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\gamma}(t)=v_{t}(\tilde{\gamma}(t)),

in the sense that each component
\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}
of the curve is in
AC([0,T],\mathbb{R})
and
\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}=v_{t}^{(k)}(\tilde{\gamma}(t))
.

Step 4
: we prove that for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
,
\tilde{\gamma}\in AC_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),D_{\infty})
. In particular, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\tilde{\gamma}(t)\in\iota(\mathcal{P})
. First, observe that for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
,
\tilde{\gamma}(t)\in\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for any
t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,T]
.
Fix
t\in[0,T]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, then

\mathtt{L}\big(\{\tilde{\gamma}\ :\ \tilde{\gamma}(t)\in\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\}\big)=\mathtt{m}_{t}(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))=1,

so we conclude because
[0,T]\cap\mathbb{Q}
is countable.

Now, for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\big(\tilde{\gamma}(t)\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
,
\tilde{\gamma}\in AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},D_{\infty})
. In fact,
\mathtt{L}
-a.e. curve
\tilde{\gamma}
and for any
s,t\in[0,T]
, it holds

\displaystyle D_{\infty}(\tilde{\gamma}(t),\tilde{\gamma}(s))=

\displaystyle\sup_{n}|\tilde{\gamma}_{n}(t)-\tilde{\gamma}_{n}(s)|\wedge 1\leq\sup_{n}\int_{s}^{t}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\tilde{\gamma}(r))|dr\leq\int_{s}^{t}\sup_{n}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\tilde{\gamma}_{r})|dr.

Moreover, notice that

\displaystyle\int\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle\sup_{n}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\tilde{\gamma}_{r})|drd\mathtt{L}(\tilde{\gamma})=\int_{0}^{T}\int\sup_{n}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\tilde{\gamma}_{r})|d\mathtt{L}(\tilde{\gamma})dr=\int_{0}^{T}\int\sup_{n}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\mathrm{x})|d\mathtt{m}_{r}(\mathrm{x})dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int\sup_{n}|v_{r}(n)(\iota(\mu))|dM_{r}(\mu)dr\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int c_{r}(\mu)dM_{r}(\mu)dr<+\infty,

where we used that
\sup_{n}|v_{r}(n)(\iota(\mu))|=\sup_{n}|B_{r}[L_{\phi_{n}}](\mu)|\leq c_{r}(\mu)
. This implies that for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
it holds that

\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{n}|v_{r}^{(n)}(\tilde{\gamma}_{r})|dr<+\infty

and thanks to the inequalities above, we have that for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
,
\tilde{\gamma}\in AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},D_{\infty})
.
The above properties show that for
\mathtt{L}
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
,
\tilde{\gamma}(t)\in\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for any
t\in[0,T]
, thanks to Lemma
B.1

Step 5
: thanks to the previous step,
\tilde{\Lambda}
is concentrated over
C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}),D_{\infty})
, thus, thanks to Lemma
C.3
, it can be seen as a probability measure over it, with its natural induced compact-open topology. Now, consider the function

(4.15)

\displaystyle\Theta:C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}),D_{\infty})

\displaystyle\to C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad\mu_{t}:=\iota^{-1}(\tilde{\gamma}(t)).

It is well-defined, and thanks to the previous considerations we are allowed to define
\Lambda:=\Theta_{\sharp}\mathtt{L}
, because
\mathtt{L}
is concentrated over curves that are absolutely continuous with respect to
D_{\infty}
, and in particular on curves that are continuous with respect to it.

By the properties of
\mathtt{L}
and the fact that
e_{t}\circ\Theta(\tilde{\gamma})=\iota^{-1}(\tilde{\gamma}(t))
, it is straightforward that
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{t}
, so that we can apply Lemma
4.8
and then for
\Lambda
-a.e.
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
it holds

(4.16)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{X}\phi_{k}(x)d\mu_{t}(x)=\frac{d}{dt}L_{\phi_{k}}(\mu_{t})=B_{t}[L_{\phi_{k}}](\mu_{t})

in the sense of distribution in
(0,T)
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

By the density of
\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{A})
in
C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it is immediate to prove that, if a curve
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies (
4.16
), then (
4.11
) holds.
∎

4.2.
Correspondence between AC curves and solution to the continuity equation

In this subsection we find a natural correspondence between solutions to the continuity equation associated with a family of
L^{p}
-derivations, and curves in
AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
.

Proposition 4.10

(From CE to AC)
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.9
, assume
p\geq 1
,
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and

(4.17)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}c_{t}^{p}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty,

i.e.
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a family of
L^{p}(M_{t})
derivations.
Then, the probability measure
\Lambda
given by Theorem
4.9
is concentrated over
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and

(4.18)

\int_{0}^{T}|\dot{\boldsymbol{\mu}}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(t)dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dt<+\infty\quad\text{ for }\Lambda\text{-a.e. }\boldsymbol{\mu}.

In particular,
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
.

Proof.

Thanks to the hypothesis
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we already know that

\int W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\delta_{\bar{x}})d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})<+\infty.

Moreover, thanks to Lemma
4.8
and Fubini’s theorem, it holds that

\int_{0}^{T}\int c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}c_{t}^{p}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty,

so it is left to prove that
\Lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is in
AC_{T}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and (
4.18
) holds.

Case
p=1
: let
\phi\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|\nabla\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Then for any
s<t
it holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi d(\mu_{t}-\mu_{s})\leq\int_{s}^{t}B_{r}[L_{\phi}](\mu_{r})dr\leq\int_{s}^{t}c_{r}(\mu_{r})dr,

so that passing to the supremum w.r.t.
\phi
on the left hand side, we have
W_{1}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})\leq\int_{s}^{t}c_{r}(\mu_{r})dr
, which implies (
4.18
). Then,
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
by definition and
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC_{T}^{1}(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
easily follows.

Case
p>1
: for
\Lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we find a vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0
is satisfied.
We know that
\Lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{\mu}
solves the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(B_{t}\mu_{t})=0
, in the sense that for all
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
\phi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds

(4.19)

\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dt<+\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt=-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)B_{t}[L_{\phi}](\mu_{t})dt.

Fix a curve
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with such properties and let
\mathcal{S}
be the collection of real valued functions from
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined as

\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}\left(\left\{(t,x)\mapsto\psi(t)\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu_{t})\ :\ \psi\in C^{1}([0,T]),\ F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\right\}\right).

Define the functional

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\mathcal{B}(H):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\psi_{k}(t)B_{t}[F_{k}](\mu_{t})dt,

where the general form for
H
is
H(t,x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}(t)\nabla_{W}F_{k}(x,\mu_{t})
.
We have:

•

\mathcal{B}
is linear (due to the linearity of all the
B_{t}
’s) and well defined, i.e. it does not depend on the representation chosen for
H\in\mathcal{S}
indeed if

H=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\zeta_{i}(t)\nabla_{W}G_{i}(x,\mu_{t})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}(t)\nabla_{W}F_{k}(x,\mu_{t}),

this implies that for each
t\in[0,T]
the Wasserstein gradient of the cylinder function
\mu\mapsto\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}(t)G_{i}(\mu)-\sum_{k}\psi_{k}(t)F_{k}(\mu)
is null in
\mu_{t}
. Then by linearity of the integral and of each
B_{t}
, it holds

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\psi_{k}(t)B_{t}[F_{k}](\mu_{t})dt-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{0}^{T}\zeta_{i}(t)B_{t}[G_{i}](\mu_{t})dt\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad=\left|\int_{0}^{T}B_{t}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\zeta_{i}(t)G_{i}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}(t)F_{k}\right](\mu_{t})dt\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}(\mu_{t})\left\|\nabla_{W}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\zeta_{i}(t)G_{i}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}(t)F_{k}\right)(\cdot,\mu_{t})\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu_{t})}dt=0;

•

for any
H\in\mathcal{S}
, it holds

|\mathcal{B}(H)|\leq\left(\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu_{t}\otimes dt;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This implies that, by Hahn-Banach theorem,
\mathcal{B}
can be extended to a continuous and linear functional on the set
L^{p^{\prime}}(\mu_{t}\otimes dt;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and it can be represented by a Borel measurable vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular,
v
satisfies

(4.21)

\|v\|_{L^{p}(\mu_{t}\otimes dt;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\left(\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dt\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{B}(H)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(t,x)\cdot H(t,x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt,

for all
H\in\mathcal{S}
. This implies that
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0
, indeed for all
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, considering function
H(t,x):=\psi(t)\nabla\phi(x)
and substituting in (
4.21
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt=-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)B_{t}[L_{\phi}](\mu_{t})dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{B}(H)=-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(t,x)\cdot\nabla\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt.

Thanks to
[
AGS08
, Theorem 8.3.1]
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in AC_{T}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

\int_{0}^{T}|\dot{\mu}|_{W_{p}}^{p}(t)dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v(t,x)|^{p}d\mu_{t}dt\leq\int_{0}^{T}c_{t}^{p}(\mu_{t})dt<+\infty.

Then,
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{p}}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
follow, respectively, from Lemma
4.8
and Proposition
3.8
.
∎

4.2.1.
Non-local vector fields

Before proceeding, we introduce the notion of
non-local vector field
, and we see how it is connected to the one of derivation. As for derivations, we introduce
‘
L^{p}
-non-local vector fields’.
We will use the notation
\widetilde{M}
and
\widetilde{M_{t}}\otimes dt
introduced in Remark
2.6
and §
2.3.1
.

Definition 4.11

(
L^{p}
-non-local vector fields)
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
p\geq 1
. We say that
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M})
-non-local vector field if

(4.22)

\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM(\mu)<+\infty.

As for derivations, we will often work with a family of non-local vector field, indexed by time
t\in[0,T]
. In particular, given a Borel family of random measures
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field is a Borel measurable function
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(4.23)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be a curve of random measures and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
non-local vector field. We say that
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
holds, if for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
it holds

(4.24)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot b_{t}(x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu),

in the sense of distribution in
(0,T)
.

Remark 4.13
.

Notice that a non-local vector field always induces a derivation. To be more specific, let
M\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M})
non-local vector field, then for
M
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the following quantity defines an
L^{p}(M)
-derivation

(4.25)

B^{(b)}[F](\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x)

where a feasible
c
is given by

c^{(b)}(\mu)=\|b(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}.

The same relation holds between
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field and
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivations. In particular, in the context of Definition
4.12
, if
p\geq 1
and
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then thanks to Proposition
4.10
, it always holds that

(4.26)

|\dot{M}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)<+\infty\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T].

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be a curve of random measures and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
non-local vector fields, with
p\geq 1
, satisfying
\partial M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
. Then, (
4.24
) is satisfied also for
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{b}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Proof.

Let
\Psi\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\phi_{i}\in C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
i\leq k
. Consider a cut-off function
\rho\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
0\leq\rho\leq 1
,
\rho(x)=1
for all
|x|\leq 1
and
\rho=0
for all
|x|\geq 2
. Then, for all
R>1
define
\rho_{R}(x):=\rho(x/R)
,
\phi_{i,R}(x)=\phi_{i}(\rho_{R}(x))
and
F_{R}=\Psi(L_{\phi_{1,R}},\dots,L_{\phi_{k,R}})\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. We know that for all
\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
it holds

\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F_{R}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b_{t}(x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F_{R}(x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt,

so we want to pass to the limit on both sides as
R\to+\infty
.
Regarding the LHS:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F_{R}(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt\right|

\displaystyle\ \leq\|\xi^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\Psi\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\phi_{i,R}(x)-\phi_{i}(x)|d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt\to 0,

thanks to dominated convergence theorem. Regarding the RHS:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b_{t}(x,\mu)\cdot\big(\nabla_{W}F_{R}(x,\mu)-\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\big)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt\right|\to 0

again by dominated convergence theorem, since
\nabla_{W}F_{R}(x,\mu)\to\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)
pointwise in
(x,\mu)
, and the domination is given by
2\|\xi\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\partial_{i}\Psi\|_{\infty}\|\nabla\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}\|b_{t}(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}
.
∎

Example 4.15
.

The curve
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
introduced in (
1.7
) solves the continuity equation
\partial M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
, with
b
as in (
1.8
). Indeed, using the notation
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
for all
F=\Psi\circ L_{\Phi}\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dN}}\Psi\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{1}(x_{i}),\dots,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{k}(x_{i})\right)dm_{t}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dN}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{j}\Psi\left(L_{\Phi}(\iota(\underline{x}))\right)\nabla\phi_{j}(x_{i})\cdot b_{t}(x_{i},\underline{x})dm_{t}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dN}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{j}\Psi(L_{\Phi}(\iota(\underline{x})))\nabla\phi_{j}(x)\cdot b_{t}(x,\iota(\underline{x}))d[\iota(\underline{x})](x)dm_{t}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot b_{t}(x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu).

Consider now an absolutely continuous curve of random measures
\boldsymbol{M}\in AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, our goal is to build a non-local vector field such that the curve solves the continuity equation associated to it. Before proceeding, we need to define some useful objects in the following:

•

given a curve
\boldsymbol{M}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, define
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that for all
F:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,1]
Borel measurable, it holds

(4.27)

\int F(t,x,\mu)d\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\mu)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(t,x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt.

It coincides with the measure already indicated as
\widetilde{M_{t}}\otimes dt
, we just use this in some contexts for the sake of notation;

•

given a measure
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, define the measure
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
such that for all
H:[0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to[0,1]
Borel measurable, it holds

(4.28)

\int H(t,\gamma,\lambda)d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}(t,\gamma,\lambda)=\int_{0}^{T}\int\int H(t,\gamma,\lambda)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda)dt.

Proposition 4.16

(From AC to CE)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in AC_{T}^{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
for some
p>1
. Then, there exists an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
non-local vector field
b:[0,T],\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(4.29)

\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)=|\dot{M}|^{p}_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}(t)\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T],

and satisfying the continuity equation
\partial M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
, in the sense of (
4.24
).

Proof.

Using the results of Section
3
, let
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\operatorname{Lift}(\boldsymbol{M})
and
\mathfrak{L}:=G_{\sharp}(\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{M}})\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{p}}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, and consider the measures
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
and
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}
defined above. Thanks to propositions
3.7
and
3.9
, we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int\int|\dot{\gamma}|^{p}(t)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)dt=\int_{0}^{T}|\dot{M}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)dt<+\infty,

which implies that the map
(t,\gamma,\lambda)\mapsto D(t,\gamma):=\dot{\gamma}(t)
is in
L^{p}(\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see Lemma
C.2
). Consider the map

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:[0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

\displaystyle\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle(t,\gamma,\lambda)

\displaystyle\mapsto(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda),

and notice that
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}=\mathcal{E}_{\sharp}\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}
. Then, thanks to
[
ABS24
, Lemma 17.3]
(see also Remark
5.11
), there exists a function
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

(4.31)

\mathcal{E}_{\sharp}(D\ \Xi^{\mathfrak{L}})=b\ \Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}.

We show that
b
is the non-local vector field we are looking for: indeed

(4.32)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=

\displaystyle\int|b(t,x,\mu)|^{p}d\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\mu)dt\leq\int|\dot{\gamma}(t)|^{p}d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}(t,\gamma,\lambda)<+\infty,

again thanks to
[
ABS24
, Lemma 17.3]
. Moreover, for any
\psi\in C^{1}_{c}(0,T)
and
F=\Psi\circ L_{\Phi}\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}}F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=\int\psi^{\prime}(t)F(\mu)d\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\mu)=\int\psi^{\prime}(t)F((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}(t,\gamma,\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)\Psi\left(\int\phi_{1}(\gamma(t))d\lambda(\gamma),\dots,\int\phi_{k}(\gamma(t))d\lambda(\gamma)\right)dtd\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\int\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{i}\Psi(L_{\Phi}((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda))\nabla\phi_{i}(\gamma(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}(t)\ dtd\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\psi(t)\nabla_{W}F\big(\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)\cdot d\big(D\ \Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}\big)(t,\gamma,\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\psi(t)\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot d\big(b\ \Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}\big)(t,x,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot b(t,x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt,

where in the second last equality we exploited the definition of
\mathcal{E}
and the characterization of
b
given by (
4.31
). Putting together (
4.26
), (
4.32
) and Proposition (
3.6
), then (
4.29
) follows.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

Notice that, thanks to (
4.29
), the vector field we built is minimal in an
L^{p}
-sense, and because of the strict convexity of
|\cdot|^{p}
for
p>1
, such vector field is unique, in the sense that any other
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field
\tilde{b}
satisfying
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(\tilde{b}_{t}M_{t})=0
and (
4.29
) coincides with
b
for
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
-a.e.
(t,x,\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

A consequence of the characterization of absolutely continuous curves with curves that solve a continuity equation, is a Benamou-Brenier-type formula for random measures.

Theorem 4.18

(Benamou-Brenier formula)
.

Let
p>1
. For all
M_{0},M_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
it holds

(4.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1})=\operatorname{min}\bigg\{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt\,:\,\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0\bigg\}.

Proof.

Thanks to (
4.26
), all the competitors for the right-hand side satisfy the inequality

\mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(M_{0},M_{1})\leq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt.

On the other hand, Lemma
3.10
gives the existence of a constant speed geodesic
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in C([0,1],\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
. Then, Proposition
4.16
gives the existence of a non-local vector field
b:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
and (
4.29
), from which it follows that the curve
(M_{t})
and the non-local vector field
b
are optimal for (
4.33
).
∎

4.3.
The tangent and cotangent bundle to
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

In this subsection, we define the tangent and cotangent bundle as closure in a suitable Lebesgue space of the Wasserstein gradient of cylinder functions. Then, following the same argument of
[
AGS08
, §8.4]
we characterize the non-local vector fields of minimal energy (see Remark
4.17
) as elements of the tangent bundle.

Before proceeding, let us recall the duality pairing map between Lebesgue spaces: given any measurable space
(X,\mathcal{F})
endowed with a finite positive measure
\sigma
, then for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
the duality pairing is defined as

(4.34)

j_{p}:L^{p}(\sigma;\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{p^{\prime}}(\sigma;\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad j_{p}(V)(x):=\begin{cases}|V(x)|^{p-2}V(x)\quad&\text{ if }V(x)\neq 0\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Definition 4.19
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty)
and
M\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and recall the definition of
\widetilde{M}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
from Remark
2.6
. Then we define, respectively, the cotangent and the tangent space of
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
at
M
as

(4.35)

\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):=\operatorname{Clos}_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\{\nabla_{W}F\ :\ F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\right\}\subseteq L^{p^{\prime}}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d});

(4.36)

\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})):=\operatorname{Clos}_{L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\{j_{p^{\prime}}\left(\nabla_{W}F\right)\ :\ F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\right\}\subseteq L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Notice that the tangent and the cotangent space are in duality by the maps
j_{p}
and
j_{p^{\prime}}
, i.e.
\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=j_{p^{\prime}}(\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=j_{p}(\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
.

Remark 4.20
.

The tangent space could be defined only considering infinitely-smooth cylinder functions, that is

\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=\operatorname{Clos}_{L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\{j_{p^{\prime}}\left(\nabla_{W}(\Psi\circ L_{\Phi})\right):k\in\mathbb{N},\,\Psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\,\Phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{k})\right\},

since any
C^{1}_{c}
function can be uniformly approximated by functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
p>1
,
M\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
b\in L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
b\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
if and only if
\|b+b^{\prime}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\geq\|b\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for all
b^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\langle b^{\prime},\omega\rangle=0
for all
\omega\in\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

In particular, for every
b\in L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a unique element
\Pi(b)\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
in the set of vector fields
b^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\widetilde{M};\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
\langle b,\omega\rangle=\langle b^{\prime},\omega\rangle
for all
\omega\in\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\Pi(b)
is the element of minimal norm in this class.

Notice that the condition that
\langle b^{\prime},\omega\rangle=0
for all
\omega\in\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is equivalent to ask that
\langle b^{\prime},\nabla_{W}F\rangle=0
for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, which, consistently with our notation, can be written in the compact form
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b^{\prime}M)=0
.

Proof.

As in
[
AGS08
, Lemma 8.4.2]
, by convexity of the
L^{p}
-norm to the power
p
and the fact that
pj_{p}(b)
belongs to its subdifferential at the function
b
, we have that
\|b+b^{\prime}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\geq\|b\|_{L^{p}}
for all
b^{\prime}
satisfying
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b^{\prime}M)=0
if and only if
\langle j_{p}(b),b^{\prime}\rangle=0
for all
b^{\prime}
as before, and by the Hahn-Banach theorem this happens if and only if
j_{p}(b)\in\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. This is equivalent to say
b=j_{p^{\prime}}(j_{p}(b))\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. The last part follows from the fact that the class of vector fields
b^{\prime}
satisfying
\langle b,\omega\rangle=\langle b^{\prime},\omega\rangle
for all
\omega\in\operatorname{CoTan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is closed and convex, so that by strict convexity of the
L^{p}
-norm there exists a unique element of minimum norm in it and by the previous characterization it belongs to
\operatorname{Tan}_{M}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
∎

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
such that
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field. Assume that the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
holds, in the sense of Definition
4.12
. Then
M_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for all
t\in[0,T]
and the following are equivalent:

(1)

b_{t}\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
;

(2)

\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)\leq|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
;

(3)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=\int_{0}^{T}|\dot{\boldsymbol{M}}|_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}^{p}(t)dt
.

Proof.

The equivalence between (2) and (3) follows from (
4.26
). Regarding (1)
\implies
(2), consider
\tilde{b}
be the
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field given by Proposition
4.16
. The goal is to show that
b_{t}=\tilde{b}_{t}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, as functions of
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t})
. By (
4.29
) and (
4.26
), it holds that

(4.37)

\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\tilde{b}_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|^{p}d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T].

Moreover, the curve
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, by assumption and by construction of
\tilde{b}
, satisfies the continuity equations
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
and
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(\tilde{b}_{t}M_{t})=0
, which implies that for all
\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
it holds

\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}b_{t}\cdot\nabla_{W}Fd\widetilde{M}_{t}\ dt=\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\tilde{b}_{t}\cdot\nabla_{W}Fd\widetilde{M}_{t}\ dt.

Localizing this equality in time, it holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}b_{t}\cdot\nabla_{W}Fd\widetilde{M}_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\tilde{b}_{t}\cdot\nabla_{W}Fd\widetilde{M}_{t}\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in[0,T],

and together with (
4.37
), since
b_{t}\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, we can apply Lemma
4.21
to conclude that
b_{t}(x,\mu)=\tilde{b}_{t}(x,\mu)
for
\widetilde{M}_{t}
-a.e.
(x,\mu)
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

Regarding (2)
\implies
(1), let us introduce the auxiliar space

\mathcal{V}:=\operatorname{Clos}_{L^{p}(\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\operatorname{Span}\left\{j_{p}\left(\xi(t)\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\right)\ :\ \xi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T),\ F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\right\}.

Following the argument of Lemma
4.21
, it is not hard to prove that a non-local vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
belongs to
\mathcal{V}
if and only if
\|v+v^{\prime}\|_{L^{p}}\geq\|v\|_{L^{p}}
for all
v^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying

\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot v^{\prime}(t,x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=0.

Thus, if we assume condition (2), because of its equivalence to (3) and the minimality given by Remark
4.17
, it holds
b\in\mathcal{V}
. Thus we conclude proving that
v\in\mathcal{V}\implies v_{t}\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. This easily follows by a pointwise argument: fix two sequences of functions
\xi_{n}\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
F_{n}\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
j_{p}(\xi_{n}\nabla_{W}F_{n})\to v
in
L^{p}(\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Up to consider a subsequence, it holds that

j_{p}(\xi_{n}\nabla_{W}F_{n})(t,\cdot,\cdot)=|\xi_{n}(t)|^{p-2}\xi_{n}(t)|\nabla_{W}F_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)|^{p-2}\nabla_{W}F_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)\to v(t,\cdot,\cdot)\quad\text{ in }L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}),

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. In particular, fixing a time
t\in[0,T]
for which the one above holds, the sequence of cylinder functions
F_{t,n}\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
defined by
F_{t,n}(x,\mu):=\xi_{n}(t)F_{n}(x,\mu)
is such that
j_{p}(\nabla_{W}F_{t,n})\to v_{t}
in
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t})
. By definition of tangent space, this proves that if
v\in\mathcal{V}
, then
v_{t}\in\operatorname{Tan}_{M_{t}}\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

4.4.
Derivations and vector fields

In this subsection, we show that any family of derivations
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is induced by a family of non-local vector fields, as in Remark
4.11
, whenever the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(B_{t}M_{t})=0
is satisfied.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
an
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivation, such that

\partial M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(B_{t}M_{t})=0
.
Then there exists an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for
M_{t}\otimes dt
-a.e.
(\mu,t)
it holds

(4.38)

B_{t}[F](\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(t,x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x),\quad\forall F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

The proof of this result is very similar to the one of Proposition
4.16
. Indeed, putting together Proposition
4.10
and Proposition
4.16
, we have a vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and for a.a.
t\in(0,T)
, it holds

\int_{\mathcal{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(t,x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}}B_{t}[F](\mu)dM_{t}(\mu).

The non-trivial part is to localize this equality with respect to the variable
\mu
, to prove (
4.38
). In the proof, we are going to see how this localization can be done in various steps, mainly in steps 2 and 3 below.

Proof of Theorem
4.23
.

Step 1
:
superposition and nested metric lifting.
Using first Proposition
4.10
and then Theorem
3.4
, we obtain a probability measure
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{A}_{p}}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
that satisfies:

(i)

(E_{t})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=M_{t}
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is such that
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda
solves the continuity equation (
4.11
), since
E_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=\Lambda
;

(iii)

\int_{0}^{T}\int\int|\dot{\gamma}|^{p}(t)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)dt<+\infty.

Consider its associated measure
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}([0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
as in (
4.28
).

Step 2
:
localization step
. Consider the function
\mathcal{F}:(t,\gamma,\lambda)\mapsto(t,\gamma(t),\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and notice that
\mathcal{F}_{\sharp}\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}=\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}
, where
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\big)
is defined such that for each
G:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to[0,1]
Borel measurable it holds

\int G(t,x,\lambda)d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\lambda)=\int_{0}^{T}\int\int G(t,\gamma(t),\lambda)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda)dt.

Since the function
\dot{\gamma}\in L^{p}(\Xi^{\mathfrak{L}})
, then thanks to
[
ABS24
, Lemma 17.3]
, it holds that there exists a function
\tilde{b}\in L^{p}(\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\mathcal{F}_{\sharp}(D\ \Xi^{\mathfrak{L}})(dt,dx,d\lambda)=\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)\ \Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}(dt,dx,d\lambda),

where
D(t,\gamma,\lambda)=\dot{\gamma}(t)
as before.

Claim
: for
\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda
,
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda
solves
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}(\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)\mu_{t})=0
.

Indeed, for any
F:\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to[0,1]
Borel measurable,
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds

\displaystyle\int F(\lambda)

\displaystyle\left[\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt\right]d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle=\int F(\lambda)\left[\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)\int_{C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\phi(\gamma(t))d\lambda(\gamma)dt\right]d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle=-\int F(\lambda)\left[\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\int_{C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\nabla\phi(\gamma(t))\cdot\dot{\gamma}(t)d\lambda(\gamma)dt\right]d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\int F(\lambda)\psi(t)\nabla\phi(\gamma(t))\cdot d\left(D\ \Xi^{\mathfrak{L}}\right)(t,\gamma,\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\int F(\lambda)\psi(t)\nabla\phi(x)\cdot\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int F(\lambda)\left[-\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\phi(x)\cdot\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)d\mu_{t}(x)dt\right]d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda).

Step 3
:
definition of the non-local vector field
. Define the continuous map
\mathcal{G}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

\mathcal{G}(t,x,\lambda)=(t,x,(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda),

and notice that
\mathcal{G}_{\sharp}\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},M}:=\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
.
At this point, consider the disintegration of
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}
w.r.t.
\mathcal{G}
, i.e. the Borel map
(t,x,\mu)\mapsto\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}_{t,x,\mu}
, that is well-defined
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
-almost everywhere. Then define the non-local vector field as

b(t,x,\mu)=\int\tilde{b}(s,z,\lambda)d\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}_{t,x,\mu}(s,z,\lambda),

which is measurable thanks to the measurability of
\tilde{b}
and of the disintegration
(t,x,\mu)\mapsto\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}_{t,x,\mu}
(see Lemma
D.1
). By construction, it’s easy to verify that
b\in L^{p}(\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Step 4
:
representation for the disintegration

\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}_{t,x,\mu}
. For any
t\in[0,T]
, disintegrate the measure
\mathfrak{L}
with respect to the map
E_{t}=(e_{t})_{\sharp}
, to obtain that there exists a family of probability measures
\{\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}\}_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
such that

\mathfrak{L}=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}dM_{t}(\mu).

Then
\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}_{t,x,\mu}=\delta_{t}\otimes\delta_{x}\otimes\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}
for
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
-a.e.
(t,x,\mu)
. Indeed for any
G:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to[0,+\infty]
Borel measurable map, we have

\displaystyle\int Gd\Xi^{\mathfrak{L},\boldsymbol{M}}=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int\left(\int G(t,x,\lambda)d\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)(x)\right)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int G(t,x,\lambda)d\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}(\lambda)d\mu(x)\right)dM_{t}(\mu)\right]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left[\int G(t,x,\lambda)d\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}(\lambda)\right]d\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left[\int\int\int G(s,z,\lambda)\ d\delta_{t}(s)d\delta_{x}(z)d\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}(\lambda)\right]d\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}(t,x,\mu),

so we conclude by mean of the uniqueness of the disintegration. In particular, for
\Xi^{\boldsymbol{M}}
-a.e.
(t,x,\mu)
, it holds

(4.39)

b(t,x,\mu)=\int\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)d\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}(\lambda).

Step 5
:
conclusion
. We are left to prove that the non-local vector field
b
satisfies (
4.38
). From Step 2 and the properties of
\mathfrak{L}
, we know that for
\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda
, for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)B_{t}[L_{\phi}]\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)(x)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)\cdot\nabla\phi(x)d\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)(x)dt,

which implies, thanks to Lemma
4.7
, that for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
it holds

B_{t}[F]\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)d\big((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\big)(x),

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
and
\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda
. At this point, for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
and
M_{t}
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can integrate both sides w.r.t.
\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}
: since
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda=\mu
for
\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}
-a.e.
\lambda
, the left hand side is constant, while on the right hand side, we can switch the order of integration to obtain, thanks to (
4.39
), that

\displaystyle B_{t}[F](\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\int\tilde{b}(t,x,\lambda)d\mathfrak{L}_{t,\mu}(\lambda)\right)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(t,x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x),

for a.e.
M_{t}\otimes dt
-a.e.
(\mu,t)
and for all
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
∎

5.
Nested superposition principle

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.2
. The strategy is similar to the one used in Section
3
to prove Theorem
1.1
. In particular, given a Borel measurable non-local vector field
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the main objects of our study are:

(i)

a curve of random measures
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
for which
b
is an
L^{1}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field and such that, according to Definition
4.12
, it holds

(5.1)

\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0;

(ii)

a probability measure
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
satisfying
\int\int_{0}^{T}\int|b(t,x,\mu_{t})|d\mu_{t}(x)dtd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})<+\infty
and concentrated over curves of measures
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
that solves

(5.2)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0;

(iii)

a probability measure
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
satisfying
\int\int a_{1}(\gamma)d\lambda(\gamma)d\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)<+\infty
(see Definition
2.3
) and concentrated over
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
that, in turn, are concentrated over
\gamma\in AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that are solutions of

(5.3)

\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma_{t},(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda).

We introduce two sets associated with a generic non-local vector field, that will play a fundamental role in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Borel map. Then, the set
\operatorname{CE}(b)\subset C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
of solutions to the continuity equation driven by the non-local vector field
b
is defined by

(5.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{CE}(b):=\bigg\{(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\ :\ \int\int|b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})|d\mu_{t}(x)dt<+\infty,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0

\displaystyle\bigg\}.

The set
\operatorname{SPS}(b)\subset\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
of superposition solutions of the particle systems (
5.3
) is defined by

(5.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SPS}(b):=\{\lambda\in

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\ :\ \int\int|b_{t}(\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda(\gamma)dt<+\infty,

\displaystyle\lambda\big(\operatorname{AC}_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)=1,\ \dot{\gamma}(t)=b_{t}(\gamma_{t},(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)\ \mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\text{-a.e.}\}.

Note that the properties of
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
listed in
(ii)
and
(iii)
, can be summarized by saying that
\Lambda
is concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
and
\mathfrak{L}
is concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
. A crucial point will be the Borel measurability of these sets, for which we refer to Proposition
5.7
and Proposition
5.8
.

Let us start noticing that the hierarchy described in the introduction between general objects
\boldsymbol{M}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
,
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
, is preserved when we require them to satisfy the conditions listed above. Before proceeding recall that

E:\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\quad E(\lambda):=((e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)_{t\in[0,T]};

\mathfrak{e}_{t}:C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \ \mathfrak{e}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mu}):=\mu_{t};\quad E_{t}:\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \ E_{t}(\lambda)=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda.

Proposition 5.2
.

If
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
, then
E(\lambda)\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
. In particular, if

\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
, then
\Lambda:=E_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda
. Then for any
\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt=\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\int\phi(\gamma(t))d\lambda(\gamma)dt=-\int\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\nabla\phi(\gamma_{t})\cdot\dot{\gamma}(t)dtd\lambda(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int\nabla\phi(\gamma_{t})\cdot b(t,\gamma_{t},(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)d\lambda(\gamma)dt=-\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\phi(x)\cdot b(t,x,\mu_{t})d\mu_{t}(x)dt.

∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
be concentrated on
\operatorname{CE}(b)
and such that

\int\int_{0}^{T}\int|b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})|d\mu_{t}(x)dtd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})<+\infty.

Then the curve of random measures defined as

M_{t}:=(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda,

solves the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
, in the sense of Definition
4.12
.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in C_{c}^{1}(0,T)
and
F=\Psi(L_{\Phi})\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)dt=\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\int F(\mu_{t})d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}(t)\psi\left(\int\phi_{1}d\mu_{t},\dots,\int\phi_{k}d\mu_{t}\right)dtd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle=
