\displaystyle-\int\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{i}\psi(L_{\Phi}(\mu_{t}))\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\phi_{i}(x)\cdot b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})d\mu_{t}(x)dtd\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu_{t})\cdot b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})d\mu_{t}(x)d\Lambda(\boldsymbol{\mu})dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\xi(t)\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)\cdot b_{t}(x,\mu)d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt.

∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
be concentrated on
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and such that

\int\int_{0}^{T}\int|b_{t}(\gamma_{t},(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda(\gamma)dtd\mathfrak{L}<+\infty.

Then the curve of random measures defined as

M_{t}:=(E_{t})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L},

solves
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
.

Proof.

Simply notice that
E_{t}=\mathfrak{e}_{t}\circ E
, and we conclude thanks to Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
.
∎

5.1.
Superposition: from
\boldsymbol{M}
to
\Lambda

Here, similarly to Section
3
, we would like to somehow invert the result of Proposition
5.3
, i.e. to define a measure
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
starting from a curve
\boldsymbol{M}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
that solves the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
. This is a simple consequence of the superposition theorem
4.9
for derivations.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an
L^{1}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector fields, according to Definition
4.11
.

Assume that the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
is satisfied.
Then there exists a measure
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
that is concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
and
(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Consider the family of
L^{1}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivations induced by the family of non-local vector fields
b
, i.e.

(5.6)

B_{t}[F](\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(t,x,\mu)\cdot\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)d\mu(x)\ \quad\forall F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

that is well-defined
M_{t}
-a.e. and for almost all
t\in(0,T)
, since

(5.7)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x,\mu)|d\mu(x)dM_{t}(\mu)dt<+\infty.

It is an
L^{1}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
family of derivations, so we can apply Theorem
4.9
to obtain
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
such that
(\mathfrak{e}_{t})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\Lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x)=B_{t}[L_{\phi}](\mu_{t})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(t,x,\mu_{t})\cdot\nabla\phi(x)d\mu_{t}(x),

for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, in the sense of distributions in
(0,T)
.
∎

5.2.
Nested superposition: from
\Lambda
to
\mathfrak{L}

Here, we want to invert the result of Proposition
5.2
, i.e. to build a measure
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
to a given measure
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
.
Let us introduce some notations: we will denote

(5.8)

Y:=[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\quad Z:=[0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).

Then, we define the maps

(5.9)

\displaystyle\kappa:C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y),\quad\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu})=dt\otimes\big(\mu_{t}\otimes\delta_{\mu_{t}}\big),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}:\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z),\quad\mathfrak{K}(\lambda):=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}.

For a fixed Borel non-local vector field
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the subset
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

(5.10)

\displaystyle\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b):=\big\{(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})\ :\ \hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Im}(\kappa),\ b\in L^{1}(\hat{\mu}),

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}=b\hat{\mu},\ \partial_{t}\hat{\mu}+\operatorname{div}(\hat{\nu})=0\big\},

where the equation
\partial_{t}\hat{\mu}+\operatorname{div}(\hat{\nu})=0
must be understood as

\forall\xi\in C_{c}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\ \int\partial_{t}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\mu}(t,x,\mu)+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)\cdot d\hat{\nu}(t,x,\mu)=0,

and, lastly, define the set
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)
as

(5.11)

\displaystyle\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b):=\bigg\{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)\ :\ \hat{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K}),\ \hat{\lambda}\big(([0,T]\times AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{c}\big)=0,

\displaystyle\hat{D}\in L^{1}(\hat{\lambda}),\ \int|\hat{D}-b\circ\hat{E}|d\hat{\lambda}=0\bigg\},

where
\hat{D}(t,\gamma,\lambda)=D(t,\gamma)
, according to Lemma
C.2
, for all
(t,\gamma,\lambda)\in Z
, and

(5.12)

\hat{E}:Z\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\hat{E}(t,\gamma,\lambda)=(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda).

Notice that
\hat{E}
coincide with the map
\mathcal{E}
already defined in (
4.30
), we just call it
\hat{E}
here for the sake of notation.

The proof of the nested superposition principle relies on the measurability of the sets
\operatorname{CE}(b)
,
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
,
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
and
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
, for which the following lemma will be fundamental.

Lemma 5.6
.

The maps
\kappa
and
\mathfrak{K}
are continuous and injective. In particular,
\operatorname{Im}(\kappa)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
and
\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K})\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)
are Borel subsets.

Proof.

The two functions are clearly continuous, and the injectivity follows by uniqueness of the disintegration with respect to the projection on the time variable. The last part of the statement follows from Lemma
A.2
and the definition of Lusin set.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Borel.
It holds

(5.13)

\operatorname{CE}(b)=\kappa^{-1}(\pi^{1}(\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b))),

where
\pi^{1}(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})=\hat{\mu}
is the projection on the first coordinate.
Moreover, the set
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})
is Borel and, in particular,
\operatorname{CE}(b)\subset C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is Borel.

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
D.1
, Lemma
D.13
and Lemma
5.6
, the sets

B_{1}:=\{\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\ :\ \hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Im}(\kappa),\ b\in L^{1}(\hat{\mu})\}

B:=\{(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})\ :\ \hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Im}(\kappa),\ b\in L^{1}(\hat{\mu}),\ \hat{\nu}=b\hat{\mu}\}

are Borel, respectively, in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Moreover, notice that
B_{1}=\pi^{1}(B)
. Then, the set

\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b):=\{(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})\ :\ b\in L^{1}(\hat{\mu}),\ \hat{\nu}=b\hat{\mu},\ \partial_{t}\hat{\mu}+\operatorname{div}(\hat{\nu})=0\}

is Borel, since it is a relatively closed subset of
B
.
Notice that the projection on the first coordinate restricted to
B
, i.e.
\pi^{1}|_{B}:B\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
, is continuous and injective, then it satisfies
\pi^{1}(B^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}\big(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\big)
for all
B^{\prime}\subset B
Borel set (see Lemma
A.2
and Corollary
A.3
).
Thus, we conclude proving (
5.13
): if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
, then
(\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu}),b\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu}))\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
; on the other hand, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\kappa^{-1}(\pi^{1}(\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)))
, then
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})=\big(\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu}),b\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu})\big)\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
, which implies that

\int_{0}^{T}\int|b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})|d\mu_{t}(x)dt=\int_{Y}|b_{t}(x,\mu)|d\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu})(t,x,\mu)<+\infty,

and

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\mu}(t,x,\mu)+\int_{Y}\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)\cdot d\hat{\nu}(t,x,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\mu}(t,x,\mu)+\int_{Y}\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)\cdot b(t,x,\mu)d\hat{\mu}(t,x,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt+\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)\cdot b(t,x,\mu_{t})d\mu_{t}(x)dt,

which means that
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfies the continuity equation, and so
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Borel. It holds

(5.14)

\operatorname{SPS}(b)=\mathfrak{K}^{-1}(\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)).

Moreover, the set
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)
is Borel and, in particular,
\operatorname{SPS}(b)\subset\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is Borel.

Proof.

The set
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
is Borel thanks to Lemma
5.6
, Lemma
C.1
, Lemma
C.2
and Lemma
D.1
, and we conclude by proving (
5.14
). If
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
, then calling
\hat{\lambda}=\mathfrak{K}(\lambda)
we have:

•

since
\lambda
is concentrated over
AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds
\hat{\lambda}\big(([0,T]\times AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{c}\big)=0
;

•

\int|D-b\circ\hat{E}|d\hat{\lambda}=0
is equivalent to
\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)

\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda
-a.e.;

•

thanks to the previous point,
\hat{D}\in L^{1}(\hat{\lambda})
is equivalent to
\int\int|b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda dt<+\infty
.

So,
\mathfrak{K}(\lambda)\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
.
On the other hand, if
\lambda\in\mathfrak{K}^{-1}(\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b))
, then
\hat{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{K}(\lambda)\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
, which means that

•

\lambda\big((AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{c}\big)=0
;

•

\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\eta)
for
\hat{\lambda}
-a.e.
(t,\gamma,\eta)
. Since
\hat{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}
, we have that
\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)

\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda
-a.e.;

•

thanks to the previous point,

\int\int|b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda dt=\int|\hat{D}|d\hat{\lambda}<+\infty.

Thus,
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and the proof is concluded.
∎

Now, everything is set to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Borel measurable. Then, there exists a Souslin-Borel measurable map
G_{b}:\operatorname{CE}(b)\to\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfying
\operatorname{Im}G_{b}\subset\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and
E\circ G_{b}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\boldsymbol{\mu}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
.

In particular, if
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
, then the measure
\mathfrak{L}:=(G_{b})_{\sharp}\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is well defined and concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
, satisfying
E_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=\Lambda
.

Proof.

Notice that, thanks to Proposition
5.2
, the map

E|_{\operatorname{SPS}(b)}:\operatorname{SPS}(b)\to\operatorname{CE}(b)

is well defined. Moreover, thanks to the finite dimensional superposition principle, Theorem
2.12
, it is surjective. Indeed for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
there exists a lifting
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda=\mu_{t}
,
\lambda
is supported over a.c. curves that solves
\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma_{t},\mu_{t})
and

\int\int|b_{t}(\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda(\gamma)dt=\int\int|b_{t}(x,\mu_{t})|d\mu_{t}(x)dt<+\infty.

In other words,
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
. Then, thanks to Proposition
5.7
and Proposition
5.8
, we can apply Theorem
A.10
to obtain a Souslin-Borel measurable map
G_{b}:\operatorname{CE}(b)\to\operatorname{SPS}(b)
, thanks to which we can define
\mathfrak{L}:=(G_{b})_{\sharp}\Lambda
, because of Corollary
A.9
. Moreover, being
G_{b}
a right-inverse of
E
, we also have the equality
\Lambda=E_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}
, so it preserves the hierarchy shown in Proposition
5.2
.
∎

5.3.
Universality in the measurable selection

In this subsection, we show a possible universal decomposition for a measurable selection map
G_{b}
, highlighting the dependence on the non-local vector field
b
in it.

We will use the same notations of the previous subsection introduced in (
5.8
)-(
5.12
), but here it will be fundamental to introduce also the following sets:

(5.15)

\displaystyle\hat{\operatorname{CE}}:=\big\{(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})\ :\ \hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Im}(\kappa),\quad\partial_{t}\hat{\mu}+\operatorname{div}\hat{\nu}=0,\quad\hat{\nu}\ll\hat{\mu}

\displaystyle\big\},

\displaystyle\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}:=\Big\{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)\ :\ \hat{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K}),\quad\hat{\lambda}\big(([0,T]\times AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{c}\big)=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 108.12054pt\hat{D}\in L^{1}(\hat{\lambda}),\quad\exists v:Y\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{s.t.}\ \hat{D}=v\circ\hat{E}\ \hat{\lambda}\text{-a.e.}

\displaystyle\Big\}.

These sets are strictly related to the sets
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
and
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
(see (
5.10
) and (
5.11
)), the difference is that they allow a general Borel field
b
instead of an a priori fixed one.

Indeed, for any element
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
there exists a Borel non-local vector field
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\hat{\nu}=v\hat{\mu}
. Moreover, since
\hat{\mu}\in\operatorname{Im}(\kappa)
, there exists a curve
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\hat{\mu}=\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu})
, and by the first condition, it also holds that
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(v_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0
. We remark that the presence of
\hat{\nu}
is also telling which vector field (defined
\hat{\mu}
-a.e.) drives the continuity equation. In particular,
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
is formally the union of all
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
as
b
varies.

Regarding the other set, any
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
can be represented as
\hat{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{1}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and by the other conditions it holds that
\lambda
is concentrated over absolutely continuous curves that solve the ordinary differential equation
\dot{\gamma}(t)=v_{t}(\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)
. As before,
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
is formally the a union of all
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
as
b
varies.

At this point, for all
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
Borel measurable we have the following commutative diagram:

(5.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SPS}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CE}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\operatorname{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{b}}

where

\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda}):=\big(\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda},\hat{E}_{\sharp}(\hat{D}\hat{\lambda})\big),\quad\kappa_{b}(\hat{\mu}):=\big(\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu}),b\kappa(\boldsymbol{\mu})\big).

In the following, we show that we can obtain a right inverse
\hat{\mathcal{G}}:\hat{\operatorname{CE}}\to\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
of the map
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
, performing again a measurable selection. Then, we infer that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b}(\operatorname{CE}(b))\subseteq\mathfrak{K}(\operatorname{SPS}(b))
for any measurable
b
, giving the diagram

(5.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SPS}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CE}(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\operatorname{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 4.26773pt\mathfrak{K}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\mathcal{G}}}

Then, composition
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b}
will be a right inverse of
E|_{\operatorname{SPS}_{b}}
.
This strategy is an alternative proof for Theorem
5.9
, providing a more refined right inverse of the map
E|_{\operatorname{SPS}_{b}}
. Moreover, the measurable selection map
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is universal, in the sense that it does not depend on
b
.

In the following, we make rigorous this strategy step by step, starting with a more general result that will be useful but could be of independent interest.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
R,S
be Polish spaces,
e:S\to R
Borel measurable and
f:S\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
a Borel function.
Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(S)
be such that
f\in L^{1}(\lambda)
. Assume that

\mathcal{H}\left(e_{\sharp}(f\lambda)|e_{\sharp}\lambda\right)=\mathcal{H}(f\lambda|\lambda),

where

\mathcal{H}(\nu|\mu):=\int\sqrt{1+\left|\frac{d\nu}{d\mu}\right|^{2}}d\mu+|\nu^{\perp}|,\quad\nu=\frac{d\nu}{d\mu}\mu+\nu^{\perp}\text{ with }\nu^{\perp}\perp\mu.

Then, calling
v:R\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
a version for the density of
e_{\sharp}(f\lambda)
w.r.t.
e_{\sharp}\lambda
, we have that

f=v\circ e\ \lambda\text{-a.e.}

Proof.

First of all, notice that

\mathcal{H}\left(e_{\sharp}(f\lambda)|e_{\sharp}\lambda\right)=\int_{R}\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}d(e_{\sharp}\lambda),\quad\mathcal{H}\left(f\lambda|\lambda\right)=\int_{S}\sqrt{1+|f|^{2}}d\lambda.

Consider the disintegration of
\lambda
with respect to the map
e
, i.e. the family of probability measures
\{\lambda_{x}\}_{x\in R}\subset\mathcal{P}(S)
such that

\lambda=\int_{R}\lambda_{x}d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x),\quad\lambda_{x}(e^{-1}(\{x\})=1.

Define the map

\tilde{v}(x):=\int_{S}f(y)d\lambda_{x}(y),

which is well-define
e_{\sharp}\lambda
-a.e. because
f\in L^{1}(\lambda)
and Borel measurable thanks to Corollary
D.6

Claim
:
v=\tilde{v}

e_{\sharp}\lambda
-a.e.

To prove the claim, it suffices to prove that
\tilde{v}
is a density for
e_{\sharp}(f\lambda)
w.r.t.
e_{\sharp}\lambda
. For any
g:R\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
it holds

\displaystyle\int_{R}g(x)\cdot de_{\sharp}(f\lambda)(x)=

\displaystyle\int_{S}g(e(y))\cdot f(y)d\lambda(y)=\int_{R}\left(\int_{S}g(e(y))\cdot f(y)d\lambda_{x}(y)\right)d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{R}g(x)\cdot\left(\int_{S}f(y)d\lambda_{x}(y)\right)d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)=\int_{R}g(x)\tilde{v}(x)d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x).

Now, for simplicity, we call
H(z)=\sqrt{1+|z|^{2}}
. Then it always holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\left(e_{\sharp}(f\lambda)|e_{\sharp}\lambda\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}H(\tilde{v}(x))d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x)=\int_{X}H\left(\int_{S}f(y)d\lambda_{x}(y)\right)d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{R}\int_{S}H(f(y))d\lambda_{x}(y)d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)(x)=\int_{S}H(f(y))d\lambda(y)=\mathcal{H}\left(f\lambda|\lambda\right),

where we used Jensen inequality. By hypothesis, we have equality, and by strict convexity of
G
the equality can hold if and only if for
(e_{\sharp}\lambda)
-a.e.
x\in R
,
f
is constant
\lambda_{x}
-a.e. In particular,
f
is constant
\lambda_{e(y)}
-a.e., for
\lambda
-a.e.
y\in S
, and looking in the definition of
\tilde{v}
, we conclude that
\tilde{v}(e(y))=f(y)

\lambda
-a.e.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

It is known that the same conclusion of the previous lemma is obtained if

(5.18)

\int_{R}|v|^{p}d(e_{\sharp}\lambda)=\int_{S}|f|^{p}d\lambda,

for
p\in(1,+\infty)
. Unfortunately, this argument fails when
p=1
, as the following counterexample shows.
Indeed, under the same assumptions of the lemma, but assuming only (
5.18
) with
p=1
, it is still true that
e_{\sharp}\eta\ll e_{\sharp}\lambda
. Anyway, it is not said that
|f|=|v\circ e|
: take
S=\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
R=\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{[0,1]\times\{-1\}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{[0,1]\times\{1\}}
,
e(x,y)=x
,
f(x,y)=1/2
is
x<0
and
f(x,y)=3/2
if
x\geq 0
. Then

\eta:=f\lambda=\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{[0,1]\times\{-1\}}+\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}_{[0,1]\times\{1\}},\quad e_{\sharp}\lambda=e_{\sharp}\eta=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{[0,1]},

which means that
v\equiv 1
. Moreover, it’s easy to verify that

\int|f|d\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{3}{2}dx=1=|e_{\sharp}\eta|(\mathbb{R}),

but it does not hold that
|f|=|v\circ e|
for
\lambda
-a.e.
y\in S
.
So, when
p=1
, we can only conclude that

f=\frac{|f|}{|v\circ e|}v\circ e\quad\lambda\text{-a.e.}

In view of this, our result is a version of the result for
p>1
, when we only have
f\in L^{1}
. Of course, the function
H(z)=\sqrt{1+z^{2}}
can be substituted with any strictly convex function
\tilde{H}
satisfying
\operatorname{lim}_{z\to+\infty}\tilde{H}(z)/x<+\infty
.

Thanks to this lemma, we can then rewrite the set
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
as

(5.19)

\displaystyle\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}:=\Big\{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)\ :\ \hat{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K}),\quad\hat{\lambda}\big(([0,T]\times AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))^{c}\big)=0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\left(\hat{E}_{\sharp}(\hat{D}\hat{\lambda})|\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}\right)=\int\sqrt{1+|\hat{D}|^{2}}d\hat{\lambda},\quad\hat{D}\in L^{1}(\hat{\lambda})\Big\}.

Indeed, thanks to Lemma
5.10
, the constraint that there exists
v:Y\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\hat{D}=v\circ\hat{E}

\hat{\lambda}
-a.e. can be rewritten by

(5.20)

\mathcal{H}\left(\hat{E}_{\sharp}(\hat{D}\hat{\lambda})|\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}\right)=\int\sqrt{1+|\hat{D}|^{2}}d\hat{\lambda}.

We are now ready to prove the following.

Theorem 5.12
.

The sets
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)
, and the map
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
are Borel measurable. Moreover,

(5.21)

\hat{\mathcal{E}}|_{\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}}:\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}\to\hat{\operatorname{CE}}

is surjective. In particular, there exists a Souslin-Borel measurable map
\hat{\mathcal{G}}:\hat{\operatorname{CE}}\to\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}=\operatorname{id}_{\hat{\operatorname{CE}}}
.
Moreover, for all
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
Borel, it holds
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b))=\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b))\subset\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
.

Proof.

Step 1
: the set
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
is measurable, since
\partial_{t}\hat{\mu}+\operatorname{div}\hat{\nu}=0
is a closed condition,
\hat{\nu}\ll\hat{\mu}
is a Borel condition (see Corollary
D.15
) and
\operatorname{Im}(\kappa)
is Borel measurable (see Lemma
5.6
).

The set
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
is Borel since: the set
\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K})
is Borel thanks to Lemma
5.6
; from Lemma
D.1
, we have that the evaluation on Borel sets is Borel and
\hat{D}\in L^{1}(\hat{\lambda})
is a Borel condition; the map
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)\ni\hat{\lambda}\mapsto\hat{D}\hat{\lambda}\in\mathcal{M}(Z;\mathbb{R}^{d})
is Borel measurable (see Corollary
D.12
), as well as the push-forward operation through
\hat{E}
(see Proposition
D.8
). Then the equality (
5.20
) is a Borel condition, again by Lemma
D.1
and the fact that
\mathcal{H}
is jointly l.s.c. (see
[
AFP00
, Chapter 2]
).

Step 2
: as before,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is the composition of Borel measurable, thanks to Corollary
D.12
and Proposition
D.8
, thus it is Borel measurable.

Moreover, it maps
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
in
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
: let
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
and
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu}):=\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})
. Since
\hat{\lambda}\in\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K})
, there exists
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\hat{\lambda}=\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}
, which implies that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes(\mu_{t}\otimes\delta_{\mu_{t}})
, where
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover, the equality (
5.20
) implies (see Lemma
5.10
) the existence of a vector field
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\hat{D}=b\circ\hat{E}
for
\hat{\lambda}
-a.e.
(t,\gamma,\eta)
. By definition of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and again by Lemma
5.10
,
\hat{\nu}=b\hat{\mu}
. Regarding the continuity equation, for all
\xi\in C_{c}^{1}((0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it holds

\displaystyle\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\mu}+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\nu}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,\gamma(t))d\hat{\lambda}+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,\gamma(t))\cdot D(t,\gamma)d\hat{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,\gamma(t))dtd\lambda(\gamma)=0.

Then,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
maps
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
in
\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
.

Step 3
: given
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
of the form
\hat{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}
, for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, using the same notation of the previous step we have

\displaystyle 0=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}

\displaystyle\xi(t,x)d\hat{\mu}+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)d\hat{\nu}=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,\gamma(t))d\hat{\lambda}+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,\gamma(t))\cdot D(t,\gamma)d\hat{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,\gamma(t))d\hat{\lambda}+\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,\gamma(t))\cdot b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\eta)d\hat{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,\gamma(t))d\lambda dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,\gamma(t))\cdot b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)d\lambda dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\xi(t,x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int\nabla_{x}\xi(t,x)\cdot b(t,x,\mu_{t})d\mu_{t}dt,

i.e.
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b))\subseteq\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
. The equality follows from the surjectivity, which we prove in the next step.

Step 4
: we are left to prove the surjectivity of the map
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
. For any
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}
, we have that
\hat{\mu}
is of the form
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes(\mu_{t}\otimes\delta_{\mu_{t}})
for some
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
\mu_{t}
solves the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0
, where
b
is a density for
\hat{\nu}
w.r.t.
\hat{\mu}
. Then we can use the finite dimensional superposition principle (Theorem
2.12
) to obtain
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
that is in
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
. It is not hard to verify that
\hat{\lambda}:=\mathfrak{K}(\lambda)\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})=(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})
.

Step 5
: we can finally apply Theorem
A.10
to obtain the existence of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}:\hat{\operatorname{CE}}\to\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
satisfying the requirements. Then, we are only left to prove that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b))\subset\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
, for which it is sufficient to show that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}|_{\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}}^{-1}(\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b))=\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b))
. Let
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
with
\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes(\mu_{t}\otimes\delta_{\mu_{t}})
for some
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and consider
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})=(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})
. Since
\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}\subset\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{K})
, there exists
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\hat{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda\otimes\delta_{\lambda}
. Moreover, since

\mathcal{H}\left(\hat{E}_{\sharp}(\hat{D}\hat{\lambda})|\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}\right)=\mathcal{H}\left(\hat{D}\hat{\lambda}|\hat{\lambda}\right),

we have that there exists a Borel measurable function
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\hat{D}=v\circ\hat{E}
for
\hat{\lambda}
-a.e.
(t,\gamma,\eta)
. In particular,
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(v)
, and thanks to Lemma
5.12
we have that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})=(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(v)
. By assumption
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)
, thus it holds
b=v
for
\hat{\mu}
-a.e.
(t,x,\mu)
. Since
\hat{\mu}=\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}
, we have that
b\circ\hat{E}=v\circ\hat{E}=D

\hat{\lambda}
-almost everywhere. In particular
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
.
∎

We are now ready to conclude the argument we presented above, stating the main result.
Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Borel measurable map. Define
\mathcal{M}_{+,b}(Y):=\big\{\hat{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\ :\ b\in L^{1}(\hat{\mu};\mathbb{R}^{d})\big\}
and the map

(5.22)

\displaystyle V_{b}:\mathcal{M}_{+,b}(Y)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\hat{\mu},b\hat{\mu}),

which is a Borel map thanks to Corollary
D.12
. Then, the map
\kappa_{b}
introduced in (
5.16
), is given by
\kappa_{b}=V_{b}\circ\kappa
, resulting Borel measurable. Recall that the (left) inverse of
\mathfrak{K}
is given by
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}=\frac{1}{T}\pi^{2}_{\sharp}
.

Corollary 5.13
.

The function
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b}
maps
\operatorname{CE}(b)
to
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and is a right inverse of the map
E|_{\operatorname{CE}(b)}
, i.e.
E\circ\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b}=\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{CE}(b)}
. In particular, for any
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
, the random measure
\mathfrak{L}:=(\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b})_{\sharp}\Lambda
is concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and satisfies the properties of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof.

For simplicity, let us call
G_{b}:=\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\circ\hat{\mathcal{G}}\circ\kappa_{b}
.
First of all, notice that
\kappa_{b}=V_{b}\circ k
is well defined and maps
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\operatorname{CE}(b)
in
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})\in\hat{\operatorname{CE}}(b)\cap\operatorname{Im}(\kappa)
. Then,
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
selects a measure
\hat{\lambda}\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\hat{\lambda})=(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})
.

Since
\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\operatorname{CE}(b))\subset\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
, then
\hat{\lambda}:=\hat{\mathcal{G}}(\kappa_{b}(\boldsymbol{\mu}))\in\hat{\operatorname{SPS}}(b)
. We need to show that
\lambda:=\mathfrak{K}^{-1}(\hat{\lambda})\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
. All the needed checks are pretty straightforward:

•

since
\lambda=\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}
, we have that
\lambda(AC_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=0
;

•

for
\hat{\lambda}
-a.e.
(t,\gamma,\eta)
it holds
D(t,\gamma)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\eta)
, which immediately implies that
D(t,\gamma)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)
for
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda
-a.e.
(t,\gamma)
;

•

\int\int|b(t,\gamma_{t},(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)|d\lambda(\gamma)dt=\int|D|d\hat{\lambda}<+\infty
.

Thus, we conclude that
G_{b}
maps
\operatorname{CE}(b)
in
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
.
We are left to show that
G_{b}
is a right inverse of
E
. Let
\lambda=G_{b}(\boldsymbol{\mu})
, then for all
f:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
Borel, it holds

\displaystyle\int f(t,\gamma(t))d\lambda(\gamma)=

\displaystyle\int f(t,\gamma(t))d\hat{\lambda}(t,\gamma,\eta)=\int f(t,x)d\hat{\mu}(t,x,\mu)=\int f(t,x)d\mu_{t}(x)dt,

where
\hat{\lambda}=\mathfrak{K}(\lambda)
and
\hat{\mu}=\hat{E}_{\sharp}\hat{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes(\mu_{t}\otimes\delta_{\mu_{t}})
. In particular,
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda=\mu_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

6.
Existence and uniqueness: the Lipschitz case

In this section, we are going to prove a uniqueness result for the solution of the continuity equation
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0,
under a Lipschitz assumption of the vector field
b
with respect to the variables
(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Here we state first a useful lemma, and then the main uniqueness theorem of this section, whose proof is divided into several parts and postponed to the next subsection.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
p\geq 1
. Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
an
L^{p}(\widetilde{M}_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field (see Definition
4.11
) satisfying
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
. The following properties hold:

(i)

if
M_{0}
is concentrated over
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the same holds for
M_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
;

(ii)

if
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, then
M_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

(i) Consider
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
given by Theorem
4.9
. Thanks to (
4.23
) and the properties of
\Lambda
, we have that for
\Lambda
-a.e.
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
it holds

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x,\mu_{t})|^{p}d\mu_{t}(x)dt<+\infty,

and
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0
. Moreover, since
(\mathfrak{e}_{0})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{0}
, we have that
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
M_{0}
-a.e.
\mu_{0}
. Then, for any
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
with these properties, consider
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
given by Theorem
2.12
, so that

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t},\mu_{0})\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int|\dot{\gamma}|^{p}(s)d\lambda(\gamma)ds=\int_{0}^{t}\int|b(s,\gamma_{s},(e_{s})_{\sharp}\lambda)|^{p}(s)d\lambda(\gamma)ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(s,x,\mu_{s})|^{p}d\mu_{s}(x)ds<+\infty

and given a transport plan
\pi_{0,t}
realizing
W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{t})
, we have

(6.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}d\mu_{t}(x)\leq 2^{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}d\pi_{0,t}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y^{p}|d\mu_{0}(y)<+\infty,

which gives that
M_{t}
is concentrated over
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Property (ii) follows from Proposition
4.10
.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
p\geq 1
. Let
\boldsymbol{M}=(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
and
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
an
L^{p}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-non-local vector field satisfying:

(1)

for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a
W_{p}
-optimal plan
\pi
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
such that

(6.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|b(t,x_{0},\mu_{0})-b(t,x_{1},\mu_{1})|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L(t)W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),

with
L\in L^{1}(0,T)
;

(2)

\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
and
M_{0}=\overline{M}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is concentrated over
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Then:

(i)

there exists a unique
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated on
\operatorname{CE}(b)
and such that
(\mathfrak{e}_{0})_{\sharp}\Lambda=\overline{M}
;

(ii)

there exists a unique
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated on
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and such that
(E_{0})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=\overline{M}
.

In particular,
\boldsymbol{M}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
is the unique solution of
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
satisfying
M_{0}=\overline{M}
.

It is worth commenting on the existence for solutions of the continuity equation for random measures: it can be recovered under Lipschitz assumptions in the spirit of our theorem (see
[
CLOS22
,
BF21
]
) or even under just Carathéodory assumptions (see
[
BF24
]
). Indeed, if given a non-local vector field
b:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can prove existence for
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0
for any starting measure
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then using a measurable selection argument and the disintegration theorem, for any
M_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we can prove existence for
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{CE}(b)
and such that
(\mathfrak{e}_{0})_{\sharp}\Lambda=M_{0}
. At this point, the superposition principle gives existence for:
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
solving
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
starting from
M_{0}
; and
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
concentrated over
\operatorname{SPS}(b)
and such that
(E_{0})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=M_{0}
.

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Even if point
(i)
follows from point
(ii)
, we prove it first using a more classical argument. Consider
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
given by Theorem
1.2
. We will prove uniqueness for the trajectories

(6.3)

\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\operatorname{div}(b_{t}(\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t})=0

for any fixed starting point
\overline{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, given
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{0},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{1}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
two solutions of (
6.3
), thanks to
[
AGS08
, Theorem 8.4.7 and Remark 8.4.8]
, we can differentiate in time the quantity
W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}^{1})
, to obtain that, given a
W_{p}
-optimal plan
\pi_{t}^{0,1}
between
\mu_{t}^{0}
and
\mu_{t}^{1}

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}^{1})=p\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{p-2}(x_{0}-x_{1})\cdot\big(b(t,x_{0},\mu_{t}^{0})-b(t,x_{1},\mu_{t}^{1})\big)d\pi_{t}^{0,1}(x_{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle\leq pW_{p}^{p-1}(\mu_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}^{1})\left(\int|b(t,x_{0},\mu_{t}^{0})-b(t,x_{1},\mu_{t}^{1})|^{p}d\pi_{t}^{0,1}(x_{0},x_{1})\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq pL(t)W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}^{1}).

Using Grönwall lemma, we conclude that for any
\overline{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a unique
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\overline{\mu}}\in C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
solution of (
6.3
). Thus, we have the following representation

(6.4)

\Lambda=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\overline{\mu}}}d\overline{M}(\overline{\mu}),

implying the uniqueness result of the theorem.

Using this result, we can already prove the uniqueness of
\partial_{t}M_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{t}M_{t})=0
given a starting point
\overline{M}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
concentrated over
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Indeed, if we had two different solutions
\boldsymbol{M}^{0}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{1}
, Theorem
1.2
would give us two different
\Lambda^{0},\Lambda^{1}\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
satisfying property
(i)
of Theorem
6.2
, which is a contradiction.

We are left with the proof of
(ii)
, that, similarly to the proof of
(i)
, passes again through the uniqueness of superposition solutions
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}(b)
with a fixed starting point
(e_{0})_{\sharp}\lambda=\overline{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, we will see how the Lipschitz assumption in Theorem
6.2
implies a Lipschitz assumption in the variable space
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, giving uniqueness of trajectories at the particle level. Before proceeding, we need some preliminary results, that are the extension to the case
p\geq 1
of (a part of)
[
CSS25
, Lemma 6.1, Theorem 6.2 and Theorem 7.6]
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\pi\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Assume
\mu_{0}
has finite support
S=\{\bar{x}_{1},\dots,\bar{x}_{N}\}
with
\delta:=\min\{|\bar{x}_{i}-\bar{x}_{j}|\ :\ i\neq j\}
and

\sup\big\{|y-x|\ :(x,y)\in\operatorname{supp}\pi\big\}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

Then
\pi
is
W_{p}
-optimal, i.e.
W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\int|y-x|^{p}d\pi(x,y)
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that the support of
\pi
satisfies the
c
-cyclical monotonicity property, with
c(x,y):=|x-y|^{p}
. Consider
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\operatorname{supp}\pi
, with
x_{0}:=x_{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i-1}-y_{i}|^{p}-|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{p}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\big||x_{i-1}-x_{i}|-|x_{i}-y_{i}|\big|^{p}-\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{p}\right]\geq 0,

because
(x_{i},y_{i})\in\operatorname{supp}\pi
, thus
|x_{i}-y_{i}|\leq\delta/2
, and

|x_{i-1}-y_{i}|=|x_{i-1}-x_{i}+x_{i}-y_{i}|\geq|x_{i-1}-x_{i}|-|x_{i}-y_{i}|\geq\delta-\delta/2=\delta/2.

∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be two measures with finite support,
\pi\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
and
\mu_{t}:=(\mathrm{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\pi
, where
\mathrm{x}^{t}(x_{0},x_{1}):=(1-t)x_{0}+tx_{1}
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

for every
s\in[0,1]
there exists
\delta>0
such that for every
t\in[0,1]
with
|t-s|\leq\delta

\pi^{st}:=(\mathrm{x}^{s},\mathrm{x}^{t})_{\sharp}\pi
is a
W_{p}
optimal plan between
\mu_{s}
and
\mu_{t}
. Moreover

(6.5)

W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})=|t-s|^{p}\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1});

(ii)

there exist
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{K}=1
such that for every
k=1,\dots,K
,
\mu|_{[t_{k-1},t_{k}]}
is a constant speed geodesic w.r.t.
W_{p}
and

(6.6)

W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{s},\mu_{r})=|r-s|^{p}\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\quad\forall s,r\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}];

(iii)

the length of the curve
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
, w.r.t.
W_{p}
, is
\big(\int|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\big)^{1/p}
.

Proof.

It is the very same of
[
CSS25
, Theorem 6.2]
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and some
W_{p}
-optimal plan
\pi\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
it holds

(6.7)

\int|b(x_{0},\mu_{0})-b(x_{1},\mu_{1})|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1}),

for some
L\in(0,+\infty)
. Then (
6.7
) holds for any transport plan
\pi\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.

Proof.

The proof is divided in two steps: first we prove the result for measures that are supported on finite sets, and then we use an approximation procedure to extend the result to all measures.

Step 1
: assume that
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
have finite support and consider a generic transport plan
\pi\in\Gamma(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Let
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{K}=1
be as in Lemma
6.4
, so that
(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k-1}},\mathrm{x}^{t_{k}})_{\sharp}\pi
is a
W_{p}
-optimal plan between
(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k-1}})_{\sharp}\pi
and
(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k}})_{\sharp}\pi
. It is also the unique optimal plan, see
[
AGS08
, Lemma 7.2.1 and Theorem 7.2.2]
. Then

\displaystyle\bigg(\int|b(x_{0},\mu_{0})-

\displaystyle b(x_{1},\mu_{1})|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\bigg)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bigg(\int|b(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k-1}},(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k-1}})_{\sharp}\pi)-b(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k}},(\mathrm{x}^{t_{k}})_{\sharp}\pi)|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\bigg)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}LW_{p}\big((\mathrm{x}^{t_{k-1}})_{\sharp}\pi,\mathrm{x}^{t_{k}}_{\sharp}\pi\big)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}L(t_{k}-t_{k-1})\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle=L\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1}).

Step 2
: let
\mu_{0}^{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(resp.
\mu_{1}^{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) have finite support and be such that
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
(resp.
W_{p}(\mu_{1}^{n},\mu_{1})\to 0
). Let
\pi_{0}^{n}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})
and
\pi_{1}^{n}\in\Gamma(\mu_{1},\mu_{1}^{n})
be
W_{p}
-optimal plans for which (
6.7
) is satisfied. Exploiting
[
ABS24
, Proposition 8.6]
, let
\sigma_{n}\in\mathcal{P}\big((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4}\big)
be such that

p^{12}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}=\pi_{0}^{n},\ p^{23}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}=\pi,\ p^{34}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}=\pi^{n}_{1},

where
p^{ij}
is the projection on both
i
-th and
j
-th coordinates. In particular,
p^{14}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}\in\Gamma(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{1}^{n})
and converges to
\pi
w.r.t.
W_{p}
. Indeed, rearranging the coordinates of
\sigma^{n}
, we have a transport plan between
\pi
and
(p_{1},p_{4})_{\sharp}\sigma^{n}
, which is
(p_{2},p_{3},p_{1},p_{4})_{\sharp}\sigma^{n}\in\Gamma(\pi,(p_{1},p_{4})_{\sharp}\sigma^{n})
, and

W_{p}^{p}(\pi,(p_{1},p_{4})_{\sharp}\sigma^{n})\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4}}|y_{2}-y_{1}|^{p}+|y_{3}-y_{4}|^{p}d\sigma^{n}(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})=W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})+W_{p}^{p}(\mu_{1}^{n},\mu_{1})\to 0.

Then, using the notation
(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\int

\displaystyle|b(x_{0},\mu_{0})-b(x_{1},\mu_{1})|^{p}d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\bigg)^{1/p}=\|b(y_{2},\mu_{0})-b(y_{3},\mu_{1})\|_{L^{p}(\sigma_{n};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|b(y_{2},\mu_{0})-b(y_{1},\mu_{0}^{n})\|_{L^{p}}+\|b(y_{1},\mu_{0}^{n})-b(y_{4},\mu_{1}^{n})\|_{L^{p}}+\|b(y_{4},\mu_{1}^{n})-b(y_{3},\mu_{1})\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L^{1/p}\left[W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})+W_{p}(\mu_{1}^{n},\mu_{1})+\left(\int|x_{1}-x_{0}|^{p}dp^{14}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}(x_{0},x_{1})\right)^{1/p}\right],

where in the last inequality we used the fact that (
6.7
) holds for
p^{12}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}=\pi_{0}^{n}
and
p^{34}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}=\pi^{n}_{1}
, and
p^{14}_{\sharp}\sigma_{n}
is any transport plan between
\mu_{0}^{n}
and
\mu_{1}^{n}
, that are finitely supported so that (
6.7
) holds as well, thanks to Step 1. Then, we conclude passing to the limit as
n\to+\infty
.
∎

The next result shows how the Lipschitz property along all the possible transport plans implies a Lipschitz property in space. For a proof, we refer to
[
CSS25a
, Theorem 4.8, (1)]
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be satisfying the hypothesis of Lemma
6.5
. Then, for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the map
b(\cdot,\mu):\operatorname{supp}\mu\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
L
-Lipschitz.

Now, we can proceed to prove point (ii) in Theorem
6.2
. Notice that all the previous lemmas apply to any non-local vector field
(x,\mu)\mapsto b(t,x,\mu)
, for any
t
such that
L(t)<+\infty
(in particular for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
). So, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the map
b(t,\cdot,\mu):\operatorname{supp}\mu\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
L(t)
-Lipschitz.

Now, let
\lambda\in\operatorname{SPS}_{b}
and consider
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\operatorname{supp}\lambda
that satisfy
\dot{\gamma}_{i}(t)=b(t,\gamma_{i}(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)
,
i=0,1
. Thanks to Lemma
6.1
, for
i=0,1
, we know that
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
t\in(0,T)
and
\gamma_{i}(t)\in\operatorname{supp}(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda
, because
\gamma_{i}\in\operatorname{supp}\lambda
. Then, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\gamma_{0}(t)-\gamma_{1}(t)|^{2}=

\displaystyle 2\big(\gamma_{0}(t)-\gamma_{1}(t)\big)\cdot\big(b(t,\gamma_{0}(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)-b(t,\gamma_{1}(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2L(t)|\gamma_{1}(t)-\gamma_{2}(t)|^{2}.

Thus, using Gronwall lemma and the continuity of the curves
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, we have
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{1}
, which implies that, defining
\overline{\mu}:=(e_{0})_{\sharp}\lambda
, it holds

(6.8)

\lambda=\lambda_{\overline{\mu}}:=\int\delta_{\gamma_{\overline{x}}}d\overline{\mu}(\overline{x}),

where
\gamma_{\overline{x}}\in C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the unique curve in the support of
\lambda
solving
\dot{\gamma}(t)=b(t,\gamma(t),(e_{t})_{\sharp}\lambda)
starting from
\gamma(0)=\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, considering
\mathfrak{L}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
given by Theorem
1.2
,
\mathfrak{L}
-a.e.
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
must be of the form (
6.8
), so that

(6.9)

\mathfrak{L}=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\delta_{\lambda_{\overline{\mu}}}d\overline{M}(\overline{\mu}),

giving the uniqueness of
\mathfrak{L}
satisfying
(E_{0})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{L}=\overline{M}
.

Remark 6.7
.

The same proof works, in the case
p=2
, only assuming monotonicity of the vector field
b
, i.e. that for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists a
W_{2}
-optimal plan
\pi
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
such that

(6.10)

\int(x_{0}-x_{1})\cdot\big(b(t,x_{0},\mu_{0})-b(t,x_{1},\mu_{1})\big)d\pi(x_{0},x_{1})\leq L(t)W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),

with
L\in L^{1}(0,T)
. Indeed, thanks to the aforementioned
[
CSS25
,
CSS25a
]
, the same proofs work under this hypothesis.

Appendix A
Lusin sets, Souslin sets and measurable selection theorem

Here we collect some results from
[
Sch73
]
and
[
Bog07
]
about Lusin and Souslin sets, that are persistently used throughout the paper. In particular, we recall (without proof) the universal measurability of Souslin subsets and a measurable selection theorem.

Definition A.1

(Lusin sets)
.

A subset
L\subset X
of a Hausdorff topological space
(X,\tau)
, is said to be a Lusin set if there exists a Polish space
(Y,\tau^{\prime})
and a continuous and bijective map
i:Y\to L
. The space
X
is said to be a Lusin space if it is a Lusin set.

Lusin spaces shares many interesting properties. Here we list the ones that are useful for our presentation and we suggest
[
Sch73
, Chapter 2]
for further reading.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Lusin space. Then
L\subset X
is a Lusin set if and only if it is a Borel set. In particular, the same holds when
(X,\tau)
is Polish.

A direct consequence of the definition of Lusin set and the previous lemma is the next corollary.

Corollary A.3
.

Let
(X,\tau)
and
(Y,\tau^{\prime})
be Lusin spaces and
f:Y\to X
continuous and injective. Then,
f(B)\subset X
is Borel for any
B\subset Y
Borel.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Hausdorff topological space and
L_{n}\subset X
a sequence of Lusin sets of
X
. Then
\bigcup L_{n}
is a Lusin set.

Definition A.5

(Souslin sets)
.

A subset
S\subset X
of a Hausdorff topological space
(X,\tau)
is said to be a Souslin set if there exist a Polish space
Y
and a continuous map
f:Y\to X
such that
f(Y)=S
. The space
X
is said to be a Souslin space if it is a Souslin set.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
be the class of all Souslin subsets of
X
and
\mathcal{S}(X)
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
.

An important tool to better understand the structure of the Souslin subsets of a given space, is the so-called A-operation (or Souslin operation). In the following theorem, we see how they are connected.

Theorem A.6
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Hausdorff topological space. Every Souslin subset
S\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
can be obtained from closed sets by means of the A-operation, i.e. for any
S\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
there exists a class of closed sets
\{C_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}\}
, where
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})
is any possible finite sequence, such that

(A.1)

S=\bigcup_{(n_{i})\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}}\bigcap_{k=1}^{+\infty}C_{n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}.

Moreover, the set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
is closed under the A-operation.

Remark A.7
.

Within this theorem, one can think that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)=\mathcal{S}(X)
, but it is known to be false. A consequence of the theorem is that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(X)
is closed under countable union and intersection. The problems are given by the complement operation: indeed,
[
Bog07
, Corollary 6.6.10]
, if both
S
and
S^{c}
are Souslin, then
S
is a Borel subset, and by
[
Bog07
, Theorem 6.7.10]
we know that there exists a Souslin set that is not Borel.

Now, we show that the Souslin-measurable sets are
universally measurable
.

Proposition A.8
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be a Hausdorff space and
\mu
a Borel, positive and finite measure over
X
. Then, the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}
of all the
\mu
-measurable subsets is closed under the
A
-operation.

In particular,
\mathcal{S}(X)\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mu}
for all Borel, positive and finite measure
\mu
, and we say that the Souslin
\sigma
-algebra is universally measurable.

In particular, we can use any Souslin-Borel measurable map to define a push-forward of a Borel measure and obtain a Borel measure, as the next Corollary shows.

Corollary A.9
.

Let
X,Y
be two Souslin topological spaces. Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
be Borel measure on
X
and
f:X\to Y
be a Souslin-Borel measurable map, i.e.
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{S}(X)
for any
B\in\mathcal{B}(Y)
. Then
\nu:=f_{\sharp}\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
is a well-defined measure over
Y
.

Finally, we state a version of the
measurable selection theorem
, see
[
Bog07
, Theorem 6.9.1]
.

Theorem A.10
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two Souslin topological spaces. Let
F:X\to Y
be a surjective Borel map. Then, there exists a
(\mathcal{S}(Y),\mathcal{B}(X))
-measurable map
G:Y\to X
that is a right-inverse of
F
, i.e.
F(G(y))=y
for any
y\in Y
. In addition, the image of
G
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(X)
.

Appendix B
The extended metric-topological structure of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}

In this section, we describe some properties of the space
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, i.e. the space of real sequences
\mathrm{x}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, endowed with two different structures:

•

the metric

(B.1)

D_{\infty}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}):=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|x_{n}-y_{n}|\wedge 1,

inducing the uniform convergence;

•

the topology
\tau_{w}
induced by the element-wise convergence, which is the topology given by the metric

(B.2)

d_{\infty}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y}):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{|x_{n}-y_{n}|\wedge 1}{2^{n}},

that is complete, and thus the topological space
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
is Polish. We will always consider on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\tau_{w}
.

This structure is related to
(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\hat{W}_{1})
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots\}\subset C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be satisfying:

(i)

\phi_{k}

1
-Lipschitz w.r.t.
|\cdot|\wedge 1
, in particular
\|\nabla\phi_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{Span}(\mathcal{A})
dense in
C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

(iii)

\hat{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{k}d(\mu-\nu)
.

The existence of such family is justified from the fact that the countable class of functions

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\left\{\phi_{k,n}(x)=\big(|x-x_{k}|\wedge 1\big)*\rho_{\frac{1}{n}},\quad k,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

satisfies the third condition, where
\{x_{k}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a countable and dense subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\rho(x)
is a mollifier and
\rho_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d}}\rho(\frac{x}{\varepsilon})
. On the other hand, the unit ball of
C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, endowed with the
C^{1}
-norm, is separable. Now, define

(B.3)

\iota:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\quad\iota(\mu)=\big(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),L_{\phi_{2}}(\mu),\dots\big).

Lemma B.1
.

The map
\iota
is an isometry between
(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\hat{W}_{1})
and
(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),D_{\infty})
. In particular,
\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is bounded and closed w.r.t.
D_{\infty}
, while it is Borel but not closed with respect to
\tau_{w}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\iota
is an isometry comes from (
(iii)
) of
\mathcal{A}
, which tells us

D_{\infty}(\iota(\mu),\iota(\nu))=\sup_{n}|L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu)-L_{\phi_{n}}(\nu)|\wedge 1=\sup_{n}|L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu)-L_{\phi_{n}}(\nu)|=\hat{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu).

Then, the set
\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
must be bounded and closed w.r.t.
D_{\infty}
. On the other hand, consider the sequence of measures
\mu_{n}:=\delta_{x_{n}}
, with
x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|x_{n}|\to+\infty
. Then,
\iota(\mu_{n})\to 0
, i.e.
\iota(\mu_{n})
converges element-wise to
0
, but the sequence given by all zeros is not in
\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, thus
\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is not closed in
\tau_{w}
. Anyway,
\iota
is continuous when endowing
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the narrow topology and
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
with
\tau_{w}
. Since it is also injective and
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
is Polish, then
\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is Borel because it is Lusin (see Lemma
A.2
and Corollary
A.3
).
∎

We proceed by introducing the cylinder functions for
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, the continuity equation over
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
and the superposition principle. For the proofs, we refer to
[
AT14
, Chapter 7]
.

Definition B.2

(Cylinder functions of
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
)
.

We say that a function
F:\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
is a cylinder function, and we write
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
, if there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Psi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
bounded and with bounded derivatives, such that

(B.4)

F(\mathrm{x})=\Psi(\pi_{k}(\mathrm{x}))=\Psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}.

Its gradient is then defined as

(B.5)

\nabla F(\mathrm{x})=\left(\partial_{1}\Psi(\pi_{k}(\mathrm{x})),\dots,\partial_{k}\Psi(\pi_{k}(\mathrm{x})),0,0,\dots\right).

Definition B.3

(Continuity equation over
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
)
.

Let
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
be a Borel vector field (w.r.t.
\tau_{w}
) and
(\mathtt{m}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
a
\text{weakly}^{*}
continuous curve of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
. Then, we say that the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mathtt{m}_{t}+\operatorname{div}(v_{t}\mathtt{m}_{t})=0
holds if

(B.6)

\int_{0}^{T}\int|v_{t}^{(k)}|d\mathtt{m}_{t}dt<+\infty\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N},

where the superscript
k
indicates the
k
-th component of
v
, and

(B.7)

\frac{d}{dt}\int Fd\mathtt{m}_{t}=\int\nabla F\cdot v_{t}d\mathtt{m}_{t},\quad\forall F\in\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})

in the sense of distribution of
(0,T)
.

Definition B.4
.

The set
AC_{w}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{\infty})\subset C([0,T],(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w}))
is the set of
\tau
-continuous curves
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
that are element-wise absolutely continuous.

Theorem B.5

(Superposition principle over
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
)
.

Let
v:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
and
(\mathtt{m}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
as in the previous definition, satisfying
\partial_{t}\mathtt{m}_{t}+\operatorname{div}(v_{t}\mathtt{m}_{t})=0
. Then there exists a probability measure
\mathtt{L}\in\mathcal{P}(C_{w}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{\infty}))
such that
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{m}_{t}
, it is concentrated over
AC_{w}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
and for
\lambda
-a.e.
\tilde{\gamma}
, it holds

(B.8)

\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}(t)=v_{t}^{(k)}(\tilde{\gamma}_{t})\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Appendix C
Measurability in the space of curves

We collect here some results concerning measurability properties of subsets of
C_{T}(Y)
, where
Y
is a Polish space and

C_{T}(Y)
is endowed with the distance
D_{d}

where
d
is a distance on
Y
generating its topology.

Lemma C.1
.

For any
p\in[1,+\infty)
, the space
AC^{p}_{T}(Y)
is a Borel subsets of
C_{T}(Y)
, both endowed with the sup distance.

Proof.

By Theorem 10.2,
[
ABS24
]
, the functional
a_{p}
is lower semicontinuous (their proof can be easily extended to the case
p\in(1,+\infty)
using again Lemma 10.1,
[
ABS24
]
). Then, the sublevel sets are closed, and by the definitions above we can write
AC^{p}
as the union of the sublevel sets at level
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then it is
F_{\sigma}
, and so it is Borel.
Regarding the case
p=1
, we refer to
[
AGS14
, §2.2]

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
D:[0,T]\times C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\big(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}\big)^{d}
defined as the pointwise and component-wise
\limsup
of the discrete derivatives, i.e.

\big(D(t,\gamma)\big)_{j}=\limsup_{h\to 0}\frac{\gamma_{j}(t+h)-\gamma_{j}(t)}{h},\quad j=1,\dots,d.

Then, the function
D
is Borel measurable and for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
concentrated over
AC_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
D(t,\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
\mathcal{L}^{1}_{T}\otimes\lambda
-a.e.
(t,\gamma)
.

Proof.

Being the
\limsup
of continuous functions,
D
is Borel. The last property follows from the fact that for
\lambda
-a.e.
\gamma
and a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, the derivative of
\gamma
in
t
exists and is finite.
∎

C.1.
Curves in
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}

Here we show a measurability result, linking the two spaces of continuous curves on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
with respect to the two topologies presented in Appendix
B
, i.e.
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},D_{\infty})
and
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
. In particular, we will need the following result.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\iota:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
be as in (
B.3
). The set
C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),D_{\infty})
is a Borel subset of
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
. Moreover, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by its subspace topology induced by
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},D_{\infty})
coincides with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by the subspace topology induced by
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
.

Proof.

The space
(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),D_{\infty})
is Polish, since it is isometric to
(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\hat{W}_{1})
, then the same holds for
C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),D_{\infty})
, with its natural compact-open topology, that we call
\pazocal{T}
. Such a topology is clearly stronger than the subspace topology induced by the larger (Polish) space
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
, that we denote by
\pazocal{T}_{w}
, so that the map

\operatorname{id}:\big(C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}),D_{\infty}),\pazocal{T}\big)\to\big(C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w}),\pazocal{T}_{w}\big)

is continuous and injective, i.e.
C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}),D_{\infty})
, as a topological subspace of
C_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
is Lusin by Definition
A.1
, and then Borel by Lemma
A.2
.
To conclude, notice that both the topology over
C_{T}(\iota(\mathcal{P}),D_{\infty})
makes it a Lusin space and they are comparable; then the induced Borel
\sigma
-algebras coincide by
[
Sch73
, Corollary 2, pp. 101]
.
∎

C.2.
Curve of random measures

Lemma C.4
.

For any
\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
it holds

(C.1)

\displaystyle W_{1,D_{\infty}}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1})=\sup\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}Fd(\mathtt{m}_{0}-\mathtt{m}_{1})\ :\ F(x)\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}),\ F\ 1\text{-}\operatorname{Lip}\text{ w.r.t. }D_{\infty}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}\hskip-5.69046pt\Psi\circ\pi_{k}d(\mathtt{m}_{0}-\mathtt{m}_{1}):k\in\mathbb{N},\,\Psi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\,\|\Psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1/2,\,\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\partial_{i}\Psi|\bigg\|_{\infty}\leq 1\bigg\}.

Proof.

We prove the first inequality, while the second one will be a byproduct of our argument.
The
\geq
inequality is trivial, so we focus on the other one. Define the projection functions

\pi^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\quad\pi^{n}(\mathrm{x})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},0,0,\dots)

\hskip-36.98866ptp^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad p^{n}(\mathrm{x})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}).

Notice that
W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})\leq W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1})
. Moreover, for all
\mathtt{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
, we have
\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}\to\mathtt{m}
weakly in duality with
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
. Indeed, for any
\mathrm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
,
\pi^{n}(\mathrm{x})\overset{\tau}{\to}\mathrm{x}
, so for any
F\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
, by the dominated convergence theorem, it holds

\int F(\mathrm{x})d(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m})(\mathrm{x})=\int F(\pi^{n}(\mathrm{x}))d\mathtt{m}(\mathrm{x})\to\int F(\mathrm{x})d\mathtt{m}(x).

Moreover,
D_{\infty}
is
\tau_{w}\otimes\tau_{w}
l.s.c.: indeed consider
\mathrm{x}^{(n)},\mathrm{y}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
respectively, converging component-wise to
\mathrm{x}
and
\mathrm{y}
, then

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to+\infty}D_{\infty}(\mathrm{x}^{(n)},\mathrm{y}^{(n)})=

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}|x_{j}^{(n)}-y_{j}^{(n)}|\wedge 1

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{j\leq m}|x_{j}^{(n)}-y_{j}^{(n)}|\wedge 1=\sup_{j\leq m}|x_{j}-y_{j}|\wedge 1,

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and for its arbitrariness we conclude.

Consider a sequence of
W_{1,D_{\infty}}
-optimal plans
\Pi_{n}\in\Gamma_{0}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})
. By the convergence of the marginals and the fact that
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau_{w})
is a Polish space, we have that the set of measures
\{\Pi_{n}\}
is tight, which implies that

\forall(n_{k})\ \exists(n_{k_{j}})\ :\ \Pi_{n_{k_{j}}}\to\Pi\in\Gamma(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1}),

where the convergence is weakly in duality with
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\tau\otimes\tau)
. Then, by
\tau_{w}\otimes\tau_{w}
-l.s.c. of
D_{\infty}
we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to+\infty}W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\pi^{n_{k_{j}}}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n_{k_{j}}}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})=

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to+\infty}\int D_{\infty}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})d\Pi_{n_{k_{j}}}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int D_{\infty}(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})d\Pi(\mathrm{x},\mathrm{y})\geq W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1}),

and by arbitrariness of
(n_{k})
we conclude that
\liminf_{n\to+\infty}W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})\geq W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1})
. Together with what was proved before, we have that

(C.2)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})=W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1}).

Now, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
define
D_{n}(x,y):=\sup_{j\leq n}|x_{j}-y_{j}|\wedge 1
and notice that

\displaystyle W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1})=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}W_{1,D_{n}}(\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0},\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \sup_{\{\Psi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\Psi\ D_{n}\ 1-\operatorname{Lip}\}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Psi(x)d\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{0}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Psi(y)d\pi^{n}_{\sharp}\mathtt{m}_{1}(y)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{n,\Psi\text{ as above}}\int\Psi(\pi^{n}(\mathrm{x}))d(\mathtt{m}_{0}-\mathtt{m}_{1})(\mathrm{x}).

The proof is then concluded by the fact that
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\ni x\mapsto\Psi(\pi^{n}(x))
is the general expression for a cylinder function from
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
, and it is
D_{\infty}

1
-Lipschitz if and only if
\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni x\to\Psi(x)
is
D_{n}

1
-Lipschitz. This is equivalent to ask that

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}\Psi|\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{osc}\Psi:=\max\Psi-\min\Psi\leq 1.

Moreover, up to considering a translation, we can substitute the condition on oscillation with
\|\Psi\|_{\infty}\leq 1/2
, which concludes the proof.
∎

In particular, the previous lemma characterizes Lipschitzianity with respect to
D_{\infty}
of cylinder functions over
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
through conditions on the function
\Psi
that represents it, that is

F=\Psi\circ\pi^{n}\text{ is }D_{\infty}\ 1\text{-Lipschitz }\iff\ \left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}\Psi|\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1\text{ and }\operatorname{osc}\Psi\leq 1.

The following shows how to use the previous lemma to have a duality formula for
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
using only duality with cylinder functions.

Proposition C.5
.

The following duality formula holds for the distance
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=W_{1,\hat{W}_{1}}
: for all
M,N\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we have

(C.3)

\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(M,N)=\sup_{F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ \operatorname{Lip}(F)\leq 1}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\mu)dM(\mu)-\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(\nu)dN(\nu),

where the Lipschitz constant has to be intended w.r.t. the
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
distance.

Proof.

It follows easily by Lemma
C.4
, indeed, thanks to (
C.1
), we can conclude

\displaystyle W_{1,\hat{W}_{1}}

\displaystyle(M,N)=W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\iota_{\sharp}M,\iota_{\sharp}N)=\sup\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\infty}}Fd(\iota_{\sharp}M-\iota_{\sharp}N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\bigg\{\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Psi(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu))dM(\mu)-\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Psi(L_{\phi_{1}}(\nu),\dots,L_{\phi_{n}}(\nu))dM(\nu)\bigg\},

noticing that if
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})
and it is
D_{\infty}

1
-Lipschitz then, again by the fact that
\iota
is an isometry, we have that
\Psi(L_{\phi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\phi_{n}}(\mu))\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

1
-Lipschitz.
∎

Remark C.6
.

We actually proved a stronger result: the Wasserstein distance
W_{1,W_{1}}
between
M
and
N
can be recovered by the cylinder functions depending only on the functions
(L_{\phi_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
(\phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are
1
-Lipschitz functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
(w.r.t.
|\cdot|\wedge 1
) such that

W_{1,|\cdot|\wedge 1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi_{n}(x)d(\mu-\nu)(x).

Moreover, the last equality in the proof of Lemma
C.4

W_{1,D_{\infty}}(\mathtt{m}_{0},\mathtt{m}_{1})=\sup_{n,\Psi}\int\Psi(p^{n}(\mathrm{x}))d(\mathtt{m}_{0}-\mathtt{m}_{1})(\mathrm{x}),

is telling us that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can consider a countable family
\mathcal{F}_{n}
of
1
-Lipschitz functions (w.r.t.
D_{n}
) such that the
\sup
is realized taking
F\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Defining the countable family
\mathcal{F}:=\cup\mathcal{F}_{n}
and taking in considerations what has been said above, we have that

(C.4)

\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(M,N)=\sup_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}F(L_{\psi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\psi_{n}}(\mu))d(M-N)(\mu).

Lemma C.7
.

Let
(M_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be an
L^{1}(M_{t}\otimes dt)
-derivation such that (
4.7
) holds. Then there exists a curve
t\mapsto\overline{M}_{t}
such that
\overline{M}_{t}=M_{t}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
and
(\overline{M}_{t})\in AC_{T}\big(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\big)\subset C_{T}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
.

Proof.

For any
F\in\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, the map
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto\int F(\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)
is
W^{1,1}(0,T)
with distributional derivative
\int B_{t}[F](\mu)dM_{t}(\mu)
. In particular, there exists
I_{F}\subset(0,T)
of full Lebesgue measure such that for all
s,t\in I_{F}

\int FdM_{t}-\int FdM_{s}=\int_{s}^{t}\int B_{r}[F](\mu)dM_{r}(\mu)dr.

Consider
\mathcal{C}\subset\operatorname{Cyl}_{c}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
the countable set in the supremum of (
C.4
) and take the full Lebesgue measure set
I=\cap_{F\in\mathcal{C}}I_{F}
. Then for any
s,t\in I
we have

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(M_{t},M_{s})=

\displaystyle\sup_{F\in\mathcal{C}}\int F\big(L_{\psi_{1}},\dots,L_{\psi_{n}}\big)d(M_{t}-M_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{F\in\mathcal{C}}\int_{s}^{t}\frac{d}{dr}\int F\big(L_{\psi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\psi_{n}}(\mu)\big)dM_{r}(\mu)dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{F\in\mathcal{C}}\int_{s}^{t}\int B_{r}[F](\mu)dM_{r}(\mu)dr\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int c_{r}(\mu)dM_{r}dr,

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
|F(x)-F(y)|\leq D_{n}(x,y)\leq\sup_{i\leq n}|x_{i}-y_{i}|
, which implies that the
1
-norm in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the gradient of
F
is bounded by
1
, i.e.

\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}F(x)|\leq 1\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

This implies that
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle|\nabla_{W}F(x,\mu)|\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}F\big(L_{\psi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\psi_{n}}(\mu)\big)||\nabla\psi_{i}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}F\big(L_{\psi_{1}}(\mu),\dots,L_{\psi_{n}}(\mu)\big)|\leq 1,

following also from the fact that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi_{n}
is
|\cdot|\wedge 1

1
-Lipschitz, so in particular they are
1
-Lipschitz w.r.t. the usual Euclidean norm
|\cdot|
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Then, there exists a curve of random measures
t\mapsto\overline{M}_{t}
that is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
W_{1,\hat{W}_{1}}
and
M_{t}=\overline{M}_{t}
for all
t\in I
.
∎

Appendix D
Measurability in the spaces of measures

In this section, we state some general results of Borel measurability in the spaces
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
,
\mathcal{M}(Y,\mathbb{R}^{d})
and their product, where
Y
is a Polish space. In
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
and
\mathcal{M}(Y,\mathbb{R}^{d})
we consider the weak topology in duality with
C_{b}
functions, and the product topology over product spaces.

D.1.
Equivalence between
\sigma
-algebras over
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)

Let
(Y,\tau)
be a Polish space. The goal of this subsection is to prove that the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
, endowed with the narrow topology, is the same as the smallest
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{S}
that makes measurable the evaluation on Borel sets, i.e. such that for any Borel set
A\subset Y
the map
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\ni\mu\mapsto\mu(A)\in\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{S}
-measurable.

Lemma D.1

(Measurability of
\mu\mapsto\int gd\mu
)
.

Let
Y
be a Polish space. For each Borel map
g:Y\to[0,+\infty]

G:\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\to[0,+\infty],\quad G(\mu):=\int_{Y}g\ d\mu

is a Borel function. In particular, the set

\mathcal{P}_{g}(Y):=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(Y)\ :\ \int_{Y}gd\mu<+\infty\right\}

is a Borel subset of
\mathcal{P}(Y)
.

Proof.

Define

\mathcal{H}:=\{h:Y\to\mathbb{R}\ :\ h\text{ is Borel and bounded, }\mu\mapsto\int_{Y}h\ d\mu\text{ is Borel}\}.

Obviously
\mathcal{H}
contains
C_{b}(Y)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{H}
is closed under monotone limits, indeed if
\mathcal{H}\ni h_{n}\to h
monotonically, then by dominated convergence theorem, for any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)

H(\mu):=\int_{Y}h\ d\mu=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{Y}h_{n}d\mu,

so
H
is the pointwise limit of Borel functions, thus it is Borel, which implies that
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Then we can apply
[
Bog07
, Theorem 2.12.9, (iii)]
to conclude that
h\in\mathcal{H}
for any bounded and Borel function
h:Y\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, by monotone convergence theorem, we conclude that
G
is a Borel function approximating
g
pointwise with
g_{n}:=g\wedge n
.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

The narrow topology over
X:=\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
is metrizable.

Proof.

Thanks to
[
Bog07
]
, Theorem 8.3.2, the narrow topology over
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
is induced by the norm

(D.1)

\lVert\mu\rVert_{BL}:=\sup\left\{\int_{Y}\phi d\mu\ :\ \phi\in\operatorname{Lip}_{b}(Y),\ \operatorname{LIP}(\phi)\leq 1\right\}.

∎

Before moving to the main goal of this section, we need a general lemma.

Lemma D.3
.

Let
Z
be a Polish space. Then
\mathcal{B}(Z)
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra under which continuous functions from
Z
to
\mathbb{R}
are measurable.

Proof.

Let
C\subset Z
be a closed subset. Define the continuous function
\delta_{C}(z):=\inf\{d_{Z}(y,z)\ :\ y\in C\}
. Notice that
C=\delta_{C}^{-1}(\{0\}).
Let
\mathcal{S}
be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra on
Z
such that all continuous functions from
Z
to
\mathbb{R}
are measurable. For sure
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{B}(Z)
. On the other hand, for any closed set
C\subset Z
, we have that
C=\delta_{C}^{-1}(\{0\})\in\mathcal{S}
, so
\mathcal{B}(Z)\subset\mathcal{S}
.
∎

We will use this lemma with
Z=\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
, where
Y
is a Polish space. To this aim, define the class of functions

\mathcal{F}:=\{F:\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)\to\mathbb{R}\ :\ F(\mu)=\int fd\mu,\ f\in C_{b}(Y)\}.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the collection of all narrowly continuous functions from
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
to
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{C}}
) be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra over
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
that makes measurable the functions in
\mathcal{F}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}
).
Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y))
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y))=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{C}}
follows from the previous lemma.
Regarding
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}
, trivially we have
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{C}}
. On the other hand, notice that the countable class of functions
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}
, introduced in
[
AGS08
, Remeark 5.1.1]
, is sufficient to describe the narrow topology
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
. This means that
\mathcal{T}
coincides with the smallest topology that makes continuous the functions in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y))=\sigma(\tau)=\sigma\left(\left\{F^{-1}((a,b))\ :\ F\in\mathcal{C}_{0},\ a<b,\ a,b\in\mathbb{Q}\right\}\right)\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}.

∎

Proposition D.5
.

Let
Y
be a Polish space and
\mathcal{S}
be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
that makes measurable the functions
\mu\mapsto\mu(A)
for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(Y)
. Then
\mathcal{S}
coincides with
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y))
.

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
D.1
, it holds that
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y))
. On the other hand, the integral of step functions is
\mathcal{S}
-measurable, and then also
\mu\mapsto\int fd\mu
is measurable for any
f\in C_{b}(Y)
. This implies that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\subset\mathcal{S}
, and thanks to the previous lemma we are done.
∎

Corollary D.6
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
X\ni x\mapsto\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
be a map taking values in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
. Then

x\mapsto\mu_{x}\text{ is Borel }\iff\ x\mapsto\mu_{x}(A)\text{ is Borel }\forall A\in\mathcal{B}(Y).

This corollary directly shows the measurability of the family of measures given by the
disintegration theorem
, that we recall here for completeness.

Theorem D.7
.

Let
Y,X
be Polish spaces,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Y)
and
e:Y\to X
a Borel function. Define
\theta:=e_{\sharp}\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
. Then there exists a family
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in X}\subset\mathcal{P}(Y)
such that

(i)

x\mapsto\mu_{x}(A)
is Borel measurable for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(Y)
;

(ii)

\mu(dz)=\int_{X}\mu_{x}(dy)d\theta(x)
;

(iii)

\mu_{x}
is concentrated on
e^{-1}(\{x\})
for
\theta
-a.e.
x\in X
.

Moreover, such a disintegration is unique, in the sense that if another family
\{\mu^{\prime}_{x}\}_{x\in X}
satisfies these properties, then
\mu_{x}=\mu^{\prime}_{x}
for
\theta
-a.e.
x\in X
.

D.2.
